      <pre>
This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 
to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 
to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 
are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 
publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 

We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain from automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attribution The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe. 

About Google Book Search 

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers 
discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web

OK THE IHTEBSECTIOK AND CONTACT OF 8UBFACS8. 

Haying giyen some simple illustxations of the methods employed in this 
paper by applying them to the geometry of plane cunres, I shall now 
proceed to apply them to surfaces. For the sake of distinctness this section 
IS divided into three parts. The first contains inyestigations of the principal 
properties of plane sections of surfaces of the second degree. The second 
traces the connexion of these properties with the relations of involution 
that exist among the points in which an arbitrary transversal meets any 
system of surfaces of the first and second degree. And the third contains 
extensions of these theorems to surfaces of the higher degrees. With two 
or three exceptions, I believe the theorems at which I have arrived to be 
entirely new. 

Plane sections of eurfaeee of the second degree.

A PROPERTY RELATIVE TO SURFACES WHICH PASS 

THROUGH THE SAME EIGHT POINTS IN SPACE. 

[.Mr, Fenwick,'] 



It is well known both to English and continental Mathematicians, that 
an infinite number of curves of the second degree can be made to pass 
through four points in a plane, and that if in each of these there be drawn 
tlic diameter of the chords that are parallel to a given line, all the diameters 
intersect in the same point. 

The existence in space of a theorem analogous to this, has been both 
afiirmed and denied by mathematicians of great eminence. My object, there- 
fore, is to inquire whether there be in space a property exactly analogous 
to this in piano, or whether it exist only for certain positions of the points, 
through which all the surfaces pass, or for certain positions of the plane, to 
which all the diameters are conjugate. 

The four points in a plane evidently con-espond to eight points in space. 
Let the following then be two of the infinite number of surfaces of the 
second degree that can be made to pass through the same eight points : — 

ti—az^+by^+cx^+2dxy-^2exz+2fi/z+2ffz+U^+2kx+\s=iO..{l) 
u=a'z^+by+c'a^+2d'x^+2eafz+2yffz+2ff'z+2h'p+2fc'x'^\^0. . (2)

the same eight points is 

u + Xu' = (3) 

Hence the equations of a diameter in reference to the surface (3), conju- 
gate to a plane z z=i my -|- «a: + j3, are (Malliemalician, vol. 1. p. 239.) 
(/+ V')z + (5 + \b') y + {d + \d') x+{h + W)

(f + ma) z + {b + mf) y -^ (d + me) X + (h + m(/) = (4 )

Now if the same values of x^ y, z satisfy all these equations, the diameters 
will obviously pass through the same point. 

Since the equations (4, 5, 6, 7) can be combined three and three in four 
different ways, we can deduce the values of x^ y^ z from them in four different 
ways, viz., from (4, 5, 6), (4, 5, 7), (5, 6, 7) and (4, 6, 7). If we use 
the first set of equations, c' and k will be wanting in the resulting expressions 
for X, y, z; if the second, c and A; ; if the third, b and h ; and if the fourth, 
b' and h'. But since the quantities a, a', b, b', etc., are absolutely indepen- 
dent of each other, the values of ar, y, z thus obtained, will be all different, 
and hence, generally^ the diameters do not pass through the same point. 

Let us next inquire whether the diameters pass through the same point for 
certain positions of the eight points. 

If we eliminate Xy y, z from the equations (4, 5, 6, 7), we shall find that 
the resulting equation of condition for which the diameters meet, is satisfied 
by either of the following relations (the equation of condition is omitted on 
account of its great length), 

a=ia\c^c\d=i d', e = ^',/=:/', g =y , k = k'} ^

az^ + by'' + cx^ + 2dxy -{- 2exz + '^Ifyz + 2^z +2ky + 2kx + \ =0,

The equation of condition for which the diameters meet, is also satisfied by 
h = h\ in addition to the relations (8). 'J'his reduces (9) to y^ = 0, which 
implies that the lines of intersection coincide, or that the surfaces touch one 
another in a plane curve. 

Similar results are obtained when the second of the relations (8) is em- 
ployed. Hence 

(1) If all surfaces of the second degree which have eight points 
common^ be cut by planes parallel to a given plane^ the diameters 
conjugate to these sections will all pass through the same pointy when 
the eight points are either in two parallel planes ^ or in the same plane. 

Next, let the given plane be parallel to one of the coordinate planes, that 
of xy suppose, or m =: 0, and n = 0. The equations therefore of the 
diameters reduce to 

/^ + *y + ^^ + * = 0,

6 = j',rf=d',/=y',* = A'&gt;

This being the equation of a cylindrical surface parallel to the given plane, 
shows that the eight points are on a cylindrical surface whose generator is 
parallel to the given plane, and whose director is a plane curve. 
The second of the relations (10) gives a similar result 
Hence, 

(2) ITie diameters conjugate to sections made by planes parallel to 
a given plane will also pass through the same pointy when the eight 
points that are common to all the surfaces lie on a cylindrical surface 
parallel to the given plane^ whose director is a plane curve of the second 
degree. 

The theorem in general was proposed in the ^* Lady's and Gentleman's 
Diary'* for 1843, but no demonstration of it has been given. It is veiy 
probable, that many of those theorems in piano that might be supposed to 
have corresponding properties in space, in respect of lines meeting in a 
point, fail in a similar way as to the general case. In a plane, two lines 
necessarily intersect one another, either at a finite or an infinite distance. 
This, however, is not the case in space, for the lines may be in different 
planes. ^ ^

NEW DEMONSTRATIONS OF THE THEOREMS OF PASCAL 
AND BRIANCHON.

If these coincide, each of them will represent the diagonal AD, .*. m = -~

enunciated ; 

(B). The diagonals joining the opposite angular points of a hexagon 
circumscribing a conic section^ pass through the same point. 

The property (A) is the well known theorem of Pascal, and (B) that of 
Brianchon. The preceding demonstrations of these elegant properties will, 
I trust, be found to possess originality, and perhaps some degi-ee of elegance. 
They were suggested to me by my solution to exercise 37 of the present work 
(vol i. p. 327—8). 

MATHEMATICAL NOTE. 

The points of intersection of all the conjugate tangents of an ellipse 
lie in the circumference of a concentric ellipse^ the axes of which are 
diagonals of the squares described on the axes of the original ellipse. 

Definition. The touching lines which are drawn through the extremities 
of two conjugate diameters of the ellipse are named in this investigation 
conjugate tangents. 

Let y=px + q {\), imdi/=p,x + q, (2),

denoted by the equation

and (8), p being supposed to have all values. Eliminating, therefore,/?

Tables I. and 11. consist of the modulus (M), and of the reciprocal of the 
modulus, together with their multiples as far as 9. The second and third 
columns of Table III. contain the Napierian and common logarithms of the 
numbers in the first column, while the logarithms in Table TV. are not those 
of the numbers in the first column, but of their reciprocals. 

2. If the logarithms of 2, 3, 5, and 7 be found by some of the methods pro-

reciprocal of the Modulus, the logarithms of the numbers in Table III., and

and proceeding as directed for Napierian logarithms. The latter seems the 
preferable plan.

We shall now consider the converse problem, namely, to find the number 
corresponding to a given logarithm, that is to determine the anti-logarithm 
of a given number. 

7. Let it be required to find the anti-logarithm of any number N to /? 
places of decimals.

exceeds it, or, if it be negative, by adding to it the greatest multiple of log 10 
that is numerically less than it, and to the result add the logarithm of the 
gi-eatest digit (Jb) that allows the sum ( — n) to be negative.

Scholium. The methods which have now been explained, were dis- 
covered nearly seven years ago. The process of art. 4 was suggested by 
perusing, in the work alluded to in art. 2, Mr. Manning's ingenious method 
of computing logarithms (of which it may be accounted a modification), an4 
this led me to consider the converse problem. It will be observed that in 
each case, the antilogarithm is resolved into factors. This mode of develop- 
ment may frequently be effectually employed in approximating to the value 
of an unknown quantity ; as an example, I may perhaps be allowed to refer 
to my "New Method of solving Numerical Equations of all Orders." 
There are many other equations besides the common algebraical ones, to 
which the same method of continuous approximation applies with advanti^. 
I shall probably solicit the insertion of some instances in a future number of 
" the Mathematician." 

J^encastle-upon' Tpni^ 
AuguBt 18/A, 1845. 

%* The Editors take this opportanity of cordially recommending to the notice of mathema- 
tical stadeots, Mr. Weddle's ingenious tract— "A New, Simple, and General Method of Solving 
Nimxerical Equations of all Orders." The method consists in a succession of transforma- 
tions, the root of the original equation being obtained in the form of a continued product, 
and it is particularly adapted to the resolution of equations having several absent terms. 
The solution of an equation of the 30th degree with all its terms is given in the work, and 
also the solution of one of the 622Dd degree, having only four terms.

34. As the coefficients of the latter of these two formula consist entirely 
of the absolute terms and their differences, it disengages us from any con- 
sideration relative to the algebraic formula or terminzts genercUia of which

This formula differs from that which is now in use, and is new to me. 
Possessing every advantage of the system of continuous approximation, and 
harmonising wi^ the other parts of that system, it seems to merit a particular 
elucidation.

Consequently the coefficients of every pure trahsformee are the same as the 
derived functions, found by taking the successive infinitesimal differences of 
the primitive or absolute term, when expressed in a general form. In our 
processes the differentiation is latent, because it accompanies substitution, 
instead of being made to precede it, as is the usual practice. 

I do not stop to notice various points of connection between finite and 
infinitesimal differences, which might now be ttie three last tabulated formula ; 
but hasten to the practical application of what has been said. 

38. In preparing to operate upon any given series according to the 
principle of equation ii (art. 33) the successive orders of differences are 
taken as usual, until thev vanish. But instead of the first, we are to select 
the last in that portion (say / terms) of the series which we employ, and in 
the several courses of differences derived from that portion. 

If a sum, of any order, is required to be found, the successive sums of the 
partial series of / terms must be found as far as the required order, and all of 
these, except the highest order being counted among the successive differences 
appertaining to it, .the last in each set is to be selected as before. 

Let the orders of differences be n in number ; their quotients by 1 , 2, 6, 24, 
etc., are to be arranged in any convenient manner in retrogade order. 

The set of multipliers by which such a series must be supposed to have 
been derived from the next previous series in art. 23 or 33, being n, n — 1, 
. .2, 1 , and the increment which the s eries i s yet to receive being a?—/ terms ; 
the multipliers for completing it are a?*-/ -f- n — 1 and its remainders when 
1 is continually deducted.

39. In this example, the whole increment 159. .is introduced by one digit 
at a time as in arts. 27 etc. Wherefore, the virtual multipliers by which the 
line of data may be supposed to have been found, being 4, 3, 2, I, {art, 23), 
those for the first course of operation are 31, 21, 1 1, 1. The next course is 
by the previous contractions reduced to an operation of the third order ; 
consequently, of the multipliers 215, 115, 15, 5, the last three only are 
employed. From this period the work is of exactly the same character as 
that of an ordinary equation. 

In fact, if the solution of the equation in art. 30 had been deduced im- 
mediately from the formula 

1 5 16 42 99 —207 

instead of being prepared by an intervening transformation, the first course of 
multipliers would then have been 46, 36, 26, 16, 6 ; and the next 363, 263, 
163, 63, 3, and so on ; unit-increments being for a short distance intermixed 
with digit-increments.

[Mr. John Datfis^ Skibbereeny County of Cork."] 

Theorem. If from any point in the plane of a triangle perpendiculars be 
drawn to the sides, and the extremities of these perpendiculars be joined two 
and two ; if the triangle thus formed be denoted by A', the triangle by joining 
two and two the points of bisection of th)e sides of the original triangle by A, 
the radius of the circumscribed circle by R, and the distance between the 
assumed point and the centre of the circumscribed circle by R' ; then 

A'_ R^— r;*

according as the assumed point is roithin or without the circumscribed circle.

circle, and AOK a diameter. Join CP and produce it to meet the circum- 
ference of the circle in D, and draw BD. Join OP and CE, and draw the 
perpendiculars AH, BI, PE, RF to the lines BC, CA, BD, QS respectively, 
and produce QP to meet BA in O. Then, since each of the quadrilaterals

63. From the Putney College Examination Papers^ July^ 1845. 

The ground plan and picture being taken as planes of projection, there 
are given two points, and a finite line by means of their projections, and a 
plane by means of its traces. If light emanate from one of the given points, 
and the given line enlightened by it cast a shadow on the given plane, it is 
required : — 

( 1 ) To construct the projections of the shadow on the planes of projection. 

(2) To construct the perspective of that shadow, the eye being in the 
second given point. 

64. Pen-and'Ink, 

When Captain Cook arrived at the Antipodes of London, he remarked in 
his journal that he was ** as far from his friends as he could be ; " was he so ? 

65, Mr, Fenwick, 

Prove that the locus of the centres of the innumerable surfaces of the 
second order that have eight points common in space, is a surface of the 
second order, and thence deduce the corresponding locus in piano.

sions on each other.

XLV. Mr. Rutherford, 

If in each side of a triangle two points be taken (either all in the sides, or 
all in the sides produced) at equal distances from the angular points ; these 
six points will be in the same conic section. 

[FiEST ^Solution. Mr, John Laws ; and similarly by Messrs, J. 
W, EliioU, Greatham ; W, H, Levy^ Shalbourne ; and Samuel BillSy 
Hawton,^ 

Let D, E ; F, G ; H, I (the diagram for both cases is easily sketched) 
be the six points taken on the sides A6, BC, CA respectively, of any plane 
triangle ABC, as specified in the enunciation. 

Then AD = BE, BF = CG, CH = AI, 
AE = BD,BG = CF, CI = AH; 
therefore AD. AE.BF.BG.CH.CI = BE.BD.CG.CF.AI.AH (1). 

Draw the lines ID, EF, GH, and let ID meet BC in L, EF meet AC in 
M, and GH meet AB in N ; then, the sides of the triangle ABC being cut by 
the transversals ID, EF, GH, we have 

AD. BL. CI = BD. AI. CL^ 

AE. BF. CM = BE. AM.CF \ (2). 

AN.BG. CH = AH. BN.Cg) 

Divide the continued product of (2) by (1) and there results 

AN. BL. CM = AM. BN. CL (3). 

Therefore the points L, M, N, range in a straight line ; and hence (converse 
of Pascal's theorem) the points D, E, F, G, H, I, lie in the same conic section.

Cor, 1. If the sides of the triangle are indefinitely diminished it be- 
comes a point ; hence if in a plane three straight lines passing through 
a point are bisected in that point, their extremities are in the same conic 
section. 

Cor. 2. If in a conic section two chords cutting one another are l&gt;i8ected 
in their point of intersection, every other chord through the same point is 
bisected ; wherefore the only point in which two chords can bisect each other 
is the centre of the curve.

[Third Solution. Mr, Jatnes Beid,'] 

The moment of inertia of a heavy particle is a function of its distance from 
the axis ; therefore the moment of inertia of a particle is not changed when 
the particle is transferred from one point to another in a direction parallel to 
the axis. Let the given circle be projected on a plane perpendicular to the 
axis, every particle in the circle being transferred to this plane in a direction 
parallel to the axis. The projection will be an ellipse of which the axis 
major is 2a, and the axis minor 2a sin a. The moment of inertia of the 
ellipse is the same as that of the circle, and if the density of the circle = 1, 

that of the ellipse = -: . Now a and h being the semi-axes of an ellipse, 

its moment of inertia about a straight line perpendicular to its«plane is 

= M. — ~ — (Earnshaw's Dynamics, Art. 183.) 
In the case we are considering, M = Tra*, and 6 = a sin a ; 

MA' = -^*(l+8in'a).

Scholium^ by iJie Proposer, 

From the contemplation of the properties of figures situate in space we 
may often deduce with great simplicity, properties of plane figures which 
it would require considerable labour to demonstrate in reference to them 
initially viewed as plane figures. The theorem of Monge relative to the 
mutual tangents to three circles is a case in point ; and Chasles has 
enumerated a few others which flow from a glance at the epures of descrip- 
tive geometry. In the Gentleman's Diary, for 1 832 is also another case of 
a theorem of some difficulty to establish by Geometry, which is at once 
apparent from the drawing of a problem in the Geometry of Monge ; and 
evidently so deduced originally by the proposer : and the theorem here given 
will funiish several properties, mutually converse of each other, in a pre- 
cisely similar manner. We have only to take advantage of the indetermi- 
nate angle formed by the planes X and Y of this theorem, and conceive the 
planes to become identical ; when we shall have the following as one of the 
theorems referred to : — 

Theokem. If two triangles afi^Ci, a-ib^Ci lying in oneplane^ have their 
corresponding sides afi^ and ajb^ intersecting in P, b^Ci and b^^ inter ^ 
secting in Ry and Cia^, CtA^, in Q, so that FtQ,B are in one line: then 
&lt;fi«2» bfii, C1C2 will meet in one point Z&gt;. 

The young geometer will do well to exercise himself in the demonstration 
of this theorem by any independent method that may occur to him : perhaps 
the transversal metlwd will furnish the simplest method that can be 
devised.

If in a hexagon described about a conic section, the first, third, and fifth 
sides be produced till they form a triangle, and in like manner the second, 
fourth, and sixth sides till they form another triangle ; then the lines joining 
the opposite pairs of the angular points of these triangles intersect upon the 
three diagonals of the hexagon.

54 SOLUTIONS OP MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 

The conditions of identity (a) form the readiest and most convenient 
means of proving any properties that may be predicated of this curious figure. 
They are the analytical expressions of the condition that the three diagonals 
of the hexagon intersect in the same point. 

[Second Solution. Mr. Thomas fTeddle.} 
. Let ABCDEF be a hexagon whose sides touch a conic section ; let the 
first, third, and fifth sides (AB, CD, EF) when produced from the triangle 
MOQ, and the second, fourth, and sixth (BC, DE, and FA) the triangle 
LNP. Since the sides of the hexagon MCNPFQ touch the conic secUon, 
its diagonals MP, QN, and CF intersect in the same point (by Brianchon's 
theorem); that is, the lines MP and QN intersect upon CF one of the 
diagonals of the hexagon ABCDEF. Also since the sides of the hexagons 
LBMOEP and LAQODN touch the conic section, it follows, that, LO and 
MP intersect upon BE, and LO and QN upon AD. 

[Thibd Solution. Mr. John Laws,'] 
Let A, B, C, D, E, F be the six angular points of the circumscribed 
hexagon, and L, M, N, O, P, Q the six angles of the two triangles formed 
by producing the alternate sides of the hexagon. Draw the lines LO, MP 
NQ, and let LO, MP intersect in R, MP, NQ, in S, and NQ, LO, in t! 
Now (Mathematician, vol i. p. 326), the six points L, M, N, O, P, Q, lie in 
a conic section ; also in this conic section we have the inscribed hexaipons 
PLOMQN, NLOQMP, OMPLNQ, whose opposite sides intersect in the 
three triads of points A, T, D; B, R, E ; C, S, F. These three triads of 
pomts therefore (Pascal's theorem) range respectively in straight lines, and 
consequently the points T, R, S are on the diagonals AD, BE, CF of the 
hexagon ABCDEF.

Galway ; and R. H. Wright, London. '

^, A, k. 

It appears from the preceding discussion, that if in any inquiry having 
reference to surfaces that have eight points common, we can shew by the 
method of arbitrary coe^ficien/8, that a property holds in each case, that is, 
when each coefficient is arbitrary, all we prove is that it is true for some 
particular positions of the eight points. It is indeed very probable, that a 
property thus investigated, is generally true ; but a safer mode of demon- 
stration in such cases is by an arbitrary multiplier (see p. 12). It will be 
obvious that if a theorem fail by this method for any one coefficient, it must 
fail in general. 

We have in this investigation, another instance of the want of analogy 
between a property in piano and its corresponding one in space (see tibe 
paper at p. 12, just referred to, and vol. i. p. 334).

I take this opportunity to notice the objections of a correspondent in 
respect of my demonstration of Pascal's hexagramme (p. 132, vol. I.), by 
poles and polars. The same result, he observes, would be got by taking any 
six points m a plane. It is true that the same result might be obtained, but 
we should not be justified in equating to zero each factor of that result as I 
have done. This we could do only when each factor is indeterminate in 
the ordinary sense, that is, when each factor involves variables that are. 
functions of each other. 

Beferring to the article in question, it will be seen, that by combining the 
equations (28) and (29) we get the equation (31) of a line through their 
intersection. Again, by combining (28) and (30) we get the equation (32) 
of a line through the intersection of (28, 30). Lastiy (to connect the sys- 
tem completely) we combine (31) and (32) and we have 

(2ay + te + rf)f?-?) = (B). 

which is the equation of a line passing through the intersection of (31 , 32). 
Now as ai and y are the current co-ordinates of the line (R), the first factor 
is indeterminate. The second is also an indeterminate factor of the kind 
specified. For the demonstration is quite general by supposing five (A, Bj 
G, £, F), of the six points of the inscribed hexagon given, and the sixth 
(D), variable in tiie curve, which we shall denote by X Y. Hence the 
point K (^1 ^i) being determined by the lines AF, CD, will involve X, Y 
only as variable quantities. Similarly, the co-ordinates Xit y^ will involve

PROPOSITION VI. PROBLEM. 
To divide a gvoen Ellipse into Jive equal Sectors. 

LSMHA I. 

In a conic section ALBMC, let two chords BA, BC he dratvn from 
the same point B, and let them he cut hy any parallel EF to the tangent 
at B ; also let DH, DK he semt-diameters parallel to the chords 
BA, BC ; then 

BA. BE : BC.BF :: DH&gt; : DK*. 

Through A and C draw the chords AL and CM parallel respectively 
to BC and BA ; produce them to meet in 
Ny and join LM. Then it is evident from 
the familiar properties of the figure, that 
BA = NC, and BC = NA. Also, since 

MN.NC : LN.NA :: DIP : DK* 

therefore, 

MN.BA : LN.BC :: DH» : DK\ .(1) 

But by Simson's Conic Sections, book iv, 
prop, xxix, cor. 2, the line LM is parallel 
to the tangent BG, and therefore it is 
parallel to EF; hence the triangles BEF 
and NML are evidently similar : therefore 

BE : BF :: NM : NL (2) 

Multiply together the corresponding terms of the analogies (l)and (2), and 
dividing the homologous terms by the quantity common to each, the re- 
sttltijig analogy is 

BA.BE : BC.BF :: Dff : DK».

XEMMA II. 

In (he ellipse of which Die the centrey let HDO, FDQ he two equal 
sectors^ the segments HOH, PQP corresponding to tliesey are also 
equal. 

For if not, let the segment PRP be equal to HOH, and draw OR, PH, 
ED ; then (Lemma to the Porism in the Mathematician vol. i. p. 123) the 
line RO is parallel to PH, and (Stone's Conic Sections, b. v. prop, iv.) the 
sectors HDO and PDR are equal, but by hypothesis the sectors HDO and 
PDQ are equal ; therefore the sectors PDQ and PDR are equal, or the part 
is equal to the whole, which is absurd ; hence the segment HOH is equal 
to the segment PQP. 

ANALYSIS OF THE FEOBLEM. 

Let it be supposed that the ellipse, of which the centre is F, is divided into 
^\e equal sectors, by semi-diameters drawn 
to the points A, 6, C, D, £ ; and let the 
adjacent points be joined, forming an in« 
scribed pentagon. Produce each of the 
semi-diameters to meet the curve on the 
opposite side in the points K, L, M,'G, H ; 
and let the adjacent points be again joined, 
forming an inscribed decagon. Draw chords 
joining the points G, M, and G, E, the for- 
mer . intersecting the semi-diameter FA in 
the point P, the latter, in the point N : from B draw a line through N meet- 
ing the curve again in M'. 

Since (Lemma ii) the segment AGB is equal to ELD, and the segment 
BHC is equal to DKC, the chord AE is parallel to a chord through B, D, 
which again is parallel to the tangent at C, and also to the tangent at M ; 
hence the segments MA and ME, and also the corresponding sectors are 
equal. In the same manner it may be proved that the other four sectors are 
divided equally by the semi-diameters drawn to the points G, H, K, L ; the 
ellipse is therefore divided into ten equal sectors by the five diameters 
AK,BL,CM,DG,EH. 

Again, the segment KDL being equal to the opposite segment BGA, 
which by hypothesis is equal to AME, therefore KDL = AME, and hence 
the chord EL is parallel to AR ; also, since the segment BG is equal to the 
segment LE, the chord GE is parallel to BL ; hence FLEN is a parallel- 
ogram, and the line NE = FL = FB. But in the two triangles BFN and 
ENF, the side FB is equal to NE ; the side FN is common, and the angle 
BFN is equal to the alternate angle ENF, therefore the triangles are equal, 
nnd the corresponding alternate angles BNF and EFN are equal ; therefore 
BN or BM' is parallel to FE or HE ; hence the segment HB = EM' = EM ; 
consequently the points M and M' coincide. 

It easily follows from what has been proved that the figure AGNM is a 
parallelogram, of which the side AG is parallel to the semi-diameter FE, 
and AM parallel to FB ; also thatGM is parallel to the tangent at A ; hence 
(Lemma i.; KA.AP : AG' :: FA'^ : FE^ and KA.AP : FA» :: AG' : FE'. 

But the diagonals GM and AN divide each other equally in P ; therefore 
KA.AP = AF.AN; also, from the properties of diverging lines cut by 
parallels, AN' : NF' :: AG' : FE', hence, by equality of ratios, AF.AN 
: AF' :: AN' : NF' ; therefore AN : AF :: AN' : NF' ; hence NF" is a 
mean proportional between AN and AF, or the semi-diameter AF is divided 



Digitized by 



Google 



ON INVOLTJTION. 



63 



at the point N in extreme and mean ratio. But the position of A being 
arbitrary, the semi-diameter FA is given, therefore the points N, P, and the 
chords GM, AG and AM are given. Also the semi-diameters FB, FC, FD 
and FE are given ; hence the semi-diameters which divide the ellipse into 
five equal sectors are given in position. 

COMPOSITION. 

Assume A, any point of the curve, and draw the diameter AK : divide the 
semi-diameter FA in N in such manner that AF.AN = NF* and therefore 
AN : AF :: AN* : NF*. Divide AN equally in P; through P draw the 
chord GM conjugate to AF, that is, parallel to the tangent at A : draw the 
diameters GD and MC; also the chords AG and AM, and parallel to these 
respectively draw the diameters HE and Bli : the ellipse is divided into five 
equal sectors by the semi-diameters FA, FB, FC, FD, FE. 

For join the points G, E and M, B. It is evident that KA. AP = FA. AN ; 
therefore by Lemma i. AF.AN : AF* :: AG' : FE«, or AN : AF :: AG' : FE' ; 
but by hypothesis AN : AF :: AN' : NF'; therefore AN : NF:: AG : FE. 
In the same manner it may be proved that AN : NF :: AM : FB ; 
therefore the chords GE and MB intersect the semi-diameter FA, and each 
other, in the point N. 

Further, in the triangles NPG and APM, the side NP is equal to AP 
and GP to MP ; also the contained angle NPG is equal to APM ; therefore 
the triangles are equal, and the corresponding alternate angles NGP, AMP 
are equtd ; hence the chords GE and AM are parallel, and the segment AG 
is equal to the segment ME. In the same manner, from the equality of the 
triangles NPM and APG, it may be proved that the chords BM and AG 
are parallel, and the segment AM equal to GB. Again, GE being parallel 
to AM, it is parallel to BL ; therefore the segment EL = GB = AM ; but 
the chord GM being parallel to the tangent at A, the segment AG = AM ; 
consequently the five segments BG, GA, AM, ME, EL, and their corres- 
ponding sectors, are equal to one another, and as each of them is equal to 
its opposite sector included between the same two diameters, it follows that 
the ten sectors are equal to one another, and consequently that the ellipse is 
divided into five equal sectors by the alternate semi-diameters FA, FB, 
FC, FD, FE. 

Scholium. It appears from the foregoing demonstration that the processes 
for dividing the circle and the ellipse into five equal sectors, are exactly 
similar: so also, it may be remarked, are those for their division into three 
equal sectors.

ON THE THEOREMS IN SPACE ANALOGOUS TO A CERTAIN 
PROPERTY OF CURVES OF THE SECOND ORDER. 
IMr. Thomas JFeddlej Newcastle.'] 
It is a known property that, 

(A) If a diameter J conjugate to a given line^ be drawn to each of the 
indefinite number of curves of the second order that pass through four 
given points in a plane, all these diameters will intersect in the same point. 

In the Lady's and Gentleman's Diary for 1843, a theorem which was 
probably considered the analogous property in space, was proposed for 
solution. It may be enunciated as follows i— 

(B) If a diameter J conjugate to a given plane ^ be drawn to each of the 
indefinite number of surfaces of the second order that pass through eight 
given points in space, all these diameters will Intersect in the same/H&gt;in/. 

This theorem is, however, true only in particular cases as has been shown 
by Mr. Fenwick at p. p. 12—14, of No. 1. Vol. II. of this work. Mr. F. 
remarks, *' It is very probable, that many of those theorems in piano that 
might be supposed to have corresponding properties in space, in respect of 
lines meeting in a point, fail in a similar way as to the general case. In a 
plane, two lines necessarily intersect one another, either at a finite or an 
infinite distance. This, however, is not the case in space, for the lines may 
be in different planes." It must, however, be borne in mind that planes in 
space may correspond to lines in piano, and that two planes '^ necessarily 
intersect one another either at a finite or an infinite distance." Hence, 
should we find that any theorem in a plane in respect of intersecting lines is 
not true in space in respect of lines^ it may be worth while to inquire 
whether it may not be true in respect of planes. Instead, then, of endea- 
vouring to find the theorem in space which is analogous to (A), by retaining 
the diameters^ and changing the given /tn^into a given /?^7^^, let us replace 
the diameters by diametral planes and consequently retain the given line. 
Hence, observing that two planes intersect in a line, we shall have to try 
the following theorem. 

(C) If a diametral plansy conjugate to a given line, be drawn to each of 
the indefinite number of surfaces of the second order that pass through eight 
given points in space, all these diametral planes will intersect in the same 
line. 



Digitized by 



Google 



CEBTAIN FBOFEBTT 07 CTTBTXS OP THE 8EC0XD OBDEB. 67 

We proceed to ascertain whether or not this theorem is true. 

Let tt = 0, 9 = 0, be the equations to two of the surfaces that pass through 
the eight points, and v! = 0, o' = 0, their diametral planes, conjugate to 
the given line. All the surfaces of the second order passing through the 
same eight points, may obviously be represented by 

tt+Xtj=0 (1) 

\ being an arbitrary multiplier. That diametral plane of (I), which is con- 
jugate to the given line, is readily found (Gregory's Solid Geometry, art. ^) 
to be 

m' + X ©' = (2) 

Now (2) is satisfied, whatever X may be, by «'= 0, and o' = 0, which 
denote two planes. The intersection of these planes will therefore determine 
a straight line through which all the diametral planes will pass. The theorem 
(C) is consequently established. 

We may observe moreover, that while two planes intersect in a line^ 
three planes intersect in a point. Hence if we can introduce another 
arbitrary multiplier into the general equation of the surfeces, so as to cause 
the equation of the diametral planes to separate into three equations of the 
first degree, we need not change the word ^^ point " in (A) into " line^^ as 
has been done in order to get (C). Now such a multiplier may be introduced 
by supposing the surfaces to pass through seven given points only, and in 
this way the theorem analogous to (A) may be enunciated as follows : — 

(D) If a diametral plane^ conjugate to a given line^ be drawn to each of 
the indefinite number of surfaces of the second order that pass through seven 
given points in space, all these diametral planes will intersect in Uie same 
point. 

This theorem is easily established. Let « = 0, t? = 0, w = 0, be three 
of the surfaces passing through the seven points ; and u' =0, «' = 0, 
w' = 0, their diametral planes conjugate to the given line. The equation of 
all the surfaces passing through the seven points may be denoted by 

u+\f)+ fiw — O (3), 

where X and /* are arbitrary multipliers. The diametral plane of (3) con- 
jugate to the given line is 

tt' -f. X©' + fiw' = (4), 

Now (4) is satisfied, whatever be X and ^, by u' = 0, ©' = 0, n' = 0, 
which denote three planes. The intersection of these planes will therefore 
determine a point, through which all the diametral planes will pass. Hence 
theorem (D) is true. 

Each of the theorems (C), (D) is as strictly analogous to (A) as (B) is, 
and they are always true, whereas (B) is not so except in particular cases. 
From this we see that while some theorems in piano have perhaps none cor- 
responding to them in space, others have more analogous properties than one.

ON SOME RELATIONS BETWEEN THE RADII OF THE 

CIRCLES WHICH TOUCH THE CIRCLES DESCRIBED ON 

THE SIDES OF A PLANE TRIANGLE. 

[Mr, Butherfard.1 

In the first volume of the Mathematictan several demonstrations are 
given of an elegant theorem with reference to the relation between the 
reciprocals of the radii of the eight circles which may in general be described 
to touch three given circles in a plane, and a particular case of the theorem 
was proposed for demonstration in the Lady's and Gentleman's Diary for 
1845. The following proof of the property, involving very neat and 
symmetrical values of the reciprocals of the several radii of the circles of 
contact, was sent to the editor of that valuable periodical, but want of space 
unavoidably precluded its insertion in the Diary for the present year. 

Our object in the subsequent paper will be the solution of the following 
problem, and thence to deduce some relations between the radii of the 
tangent circles. 

FBOBLEM. 

To determine the values of the radii of the circles described to touch 
the circles whose diameters coincide in magnitude and position with 
the three sides of a plane triangle. 

Let A, B, C be the centres of the three circles described on EF, FD, 
DE, the sides of the triangle DEF, and O the centre of the circle which 
touches the three other circles internally. Draw AB, BC, CA, OA, OB, OC, 
and let BC = a, CA = ft, AB = ^, 2^ = a 4-/&gt; 4- ^; the radius of the

(ioogk 



70 RELATIONS BETWEEN THE RADIt OF CERTAIN CIRCLES. 

circle inscribed in the triangle ADC = r, and adopt the usual notation with 
respect to the circumscribed and escribed circles. Let also »i, #s, ft be

72 XELATI0M8 BETWEEN THE RADII OF CERTAIN CIRCLES. 

Thus we have investigated simple and symmetrical expressions for the 
values of either the reciprocals of the radii, or the radii themselves, of the 
eight tangent circles. We shall now deduce some relations between these 
radii or their reciprocals. 

The radii of the tangent circles being functions of the three sides of 
the triangle ABC, it is obvious that if the quantities a, ft, c, be eliminated 
from the eiffhi equations (A), (B), or (C), (D), we shall have five resulting 
equations involving simply the eight radii of the tangent circles, and conse- 
quently there Bvej^ve independent relations between the several radii. The 
elimination of a, b, c from these equations will be attended with considerable 
difficulty ; but there are two instances in which it can easily be effected, 
and these are given below. 

It will be necessary, however, first to show that

These two relations (£), (F) between the radii of the eight tangent circles 
may be thus enunciated. 

(£). If circles be described on the three sides of a plane triangle^ 
as diameters, and circles be described to totich thenty tmce the product 
of the radii of the circles which totich the three given circles internally 
and externally divided by the difference of their radiij is equal to the 
sum of the harmonic means between the radii of the three pairs of circles 
which touch only one of the given circles internally and externally. 

(F). If circles be described on the three sides of a plane triangle 
as diameters^ and circles be described to touch them^ the sum of the 
reciprocals of the radii of the three circles which touch only one of the 
given circles externally y is equal to the sum of the reciprocals of the 
radii of the other five. 

The investigations of these two neat relations have offered little difficulty ; 
but the complete elimination of the sides of the triangle from the eight 
equation (C, D), does not appear to be easily effected. If, however, the 
remaining three relations should be communicated to us we would avail 
ourselves of an early opportunity of placiug them on record.

CONNECTION BETWEEN THE FORMULAE IN PLANE AND 
SPHERICAL TRIGONOMETRY. 

In any spherical triangle whose angles are A, B, C, and sides a, 6, c^ 

cos a = cos b cos ^ -|- sin 6 sin ^ cos A (A)

— da sina = — db sinb cose— &lt;2a sin^ cosb-^^db cosb sin^+c^ cos^ sin6)cosA. 
Now as a, &amp;, c diminbh, the triangle approaches to a plane triangle ; the 
ultimate values of cosa, cos6, cos e are unity, and those of sin a, sin 6, 
sine, a, b, e, da^ db, and dc tend to equality. Hence for da^ db, do and 
sin a, sin b, sin c, writing a^bfC; and for cos a, cos b, cos c, writing unity,

trigonometry.

ON SURFACES OF THE SECOND ORDER THAT HAVE THE 

SAME CURVE OF INTERSECTION .♦ 

iMr, Fenwiek,] 

1 . jlll surfaces of the second order which pass through the same 
eight points huve in general tlis same curve of intersection. 

For let tt = OandM'=0, 

be the equations of any two surfaces of the second order, passing through 
the same eight points ; then \ being an arbitrary multiplier, every third 
surface passing through the same eight pc^nts has for its equation 

u + \tt = 0. 

Now as this equation is satisfied by the co-ordinates of all the points 
which satisfy at the same time the equations « = 0, u'= 0, it follows, that 
this third surface contains all the points common to the other two surfaces, 
that is, it contains the curve of intersection of those surfaces. 

2. If any number of surfaces of the second order intersect in a 
plane curve, their second intersection (iftJieyhave one) is also a plane 
curve.] 

Let

3. To find the locus of the centres of the innumerable surfaces of 
the second order that have eight points common in space.

of the arbitrary constant \. That surface is therefore generated by a 
straight line. It would be easy to shew also that it is centred^ and hence 
it is the hyperhoUnd of one sheet ^ as that is the only central surface of the 
second order that admits of a rectilinear generator. 

In a similar way we might shew that the second of the equations (4) is 
the hyperboloid of one sheet. It follows, therefore, that the centres under 
consideration lie on the intersection of two hyperboloids of one sheet. 

We shall deduce one or two properties from the general one, which may 
be interesting to the student. 
Let the surfaces be similar y or 

a:=za\ ft = y, &lt;? = &lt;?', £? = J', tf = e\ f—f'\ 

the two equations (4) then become planeSy which intersect in a line^ and 
as all spheres are similar ^ we have the following corollaries : 

Cor, I. The centres of all similar surfaces of the second order that 
have the same curve of intersection^ are in the same straight line. 

Cor. 2. If any number of spheres intersect in the same circle^ their 
centres mill be in a straight line.

ON THE TANGENTS OF ALGEBRAIC CURVES. 
[Mr, James Meidy Prior's Salfard, Wanmckshire.} 
If in an equation involving two variables, Apq represent the coefidcient of 
^y &gt; a plane curve of the n^ degree will be expressed by the equation 

^{Ap^y^)=0 .(1) 

in which ^ and q are taken successively equal to any two whole positive 
numbers whose sum does not exceed n. The equation to a straight line is 

f/ =ztnaf + c (2) 

Substituting in (I) ma; + c for y, the roots of the resulting equation are 
the abscissas of the points in which the straight line meets the curve. 
When two of the roots are equal they give a point at which the straight line 
touches the curve. 

If both the curve and straight line pass through the origin of co-ordinates 
the constant terms in (1) and (2) vanish, and if at £e same time the 
straight line is a tangent at the origin, then, when miv is substituted for y in 
(1), there must be two roots equal to zero, which cannot be the case unless 
the coefiSicient of the first power of a vanish. 

Let (x\y'] be a point in a curve the tangent at which is to be determined. 
Remove the origin to (a?',y') by substituting m (1) x'+af for a:, and y'+f/ 
for y. Then the value of m will be determined by substituting mx for y, 
and equating the co-efficient of a with zero. 
By the binomial theorem and multiplication 

(x'+af)P (y'+y)9 = x'p y'« + px'v-^ y'iix+ qx'fy'^-^ y+ . . . . (3) 
Substituting the second member of (3) for zP y^ in (1), we find the terms 
involving the first powers of x and y to be 

^{Apgpx'P-^ y'9)xy S(A^^a?V*-^ )y- 
Put mx for y, and equate the sum of these with zero ; then we find at once 

^{Ap,px'P-^y'^) 
*"- ^A^x'^y'^^) 
An expression indicating an operation identical with differentiation. 

Having found m, the value of c in (2) is determined by the condition that 
the line passes through (x',y) ; hence 

c = y' — mx'. 
The values of m and c thus found are such as render the line (2) a tangent 
to the curve (1) at the point x\y'.

MODERN GEOMETRY. 
\Mr. T. S. Davies, F. B. 5..] 

(Continued from p. 856, toI. i.) 

chapter. iii. 
The Rule Surfaces of the Second Order. 

XXV. 

The first regular attempt to classify surfaces of the second order was 
made by Euler : but it is to Hachette that we owe our present complete 
view of the subject. Amongst ourselves these surfaces are scarcely known 
(except the simplest cases) otherwise than as the surfaces represented by 
the equation of the second degree ; and I know of no English work in which 
these surfaces are rigorously discussed, or any genesis given of them when 
viewed as surfaces formed according to specified laws. The cases of the 
ellipsoid, paraboloid and hyperboloid of two sheets, are indeed familar 
enough, under the name of conoids, when of revolution ; and the extension 
to the cases where they are not of revolution is easy enough of conception, 
by considering all the ordinates in reference to one of the meridian planes 
to be altered in a given ratio. Yet of even these figures, it would be 
impossible to point out an English work which developes the general pro- 



Digitized by 



Google 



92 XOBSBK GSOMETET. 

perties with syBtematic precision, by analysis or geometry. This is the more 
remarkable, as such a development by either method is attended with no 
extraordinary difficulty. 

There are, however, two other surfaces of the second order, to which we 
scarcely ever find any distinct allusion in our books ; and yet they are sur- 
faces which are not only- necessary for completing the series of the second 
order, but otherwise possessed of properties so remarkable as to render them 
peculiarly attractive to the geometer. An elementary view of these will 
constitute the present chapter of modem geometry. 

XXVI. 

In order to discuss the surfaces of the second order geometrically, a 
geometrical genesis of them is essential. 

1. Draw any tangent to the ellipse, parabola or hyperbola, and through 
this a plane perpendicular to that of the figure ; let this plane move parallel 
to itself cutting the figure, and upon the chord so made in each position 
let a circle be described: then this variable circumference will re- 
spectively describe the elltpsatd^ elliptic paraholotd and the hyperboloid 
^tvfo sheets. 

This genesis is very analogous to that of an oblique cone ; and in con- 
tinuation of the same analogy, we may remark that the same surface may be 
described by the circle taken in one other special position, like that of the 
subcontrary section of the oblique cone. These circular sections have been 
aptly called by Chasles, the cpelieal sections^ and the planes parallel to 
them cyclical planes. They possess many curious and interesting pro- 
perties. 

2. If there be given in space three straight lines, no two of which are in 
the same plane, and upon these as directrices, another line constantly move, 
it will generate a surface of the second order, which (for a reason to be 
presently given) is called the hyperboloid of one sheet. 

3. If these three lines are parallel to the same plane, the surface gene- 
rated by the fourth line is called the hyperhoUc parabckHd. 

The last-named surface is sometimes defined by another mode of genesis : 
viz. by supposing the generating line to move upon two given lines, and be 
always parallel to a given plane. This, as will appear further on, is merely 
a consequence of the former hypothesis of the three directrices being parallel 
to another given plane. 

XXVII. 

To afford a general idea of the hyperboloid of one sheet, we will suppose 
the hyperbola to revolve about its imaginary axis Tthat is, one at right angles 
to the real axis of the curve, and passing through its centre) which is very 
easy of conception. This hyperbola will describe the hyperboloid oi revolu- 
tion of one sheet. It will require little effort of imagination to conceive that 
a line may rest wholly upon this surface in a position oblique to the axis. If 
the asymptotic anffle m which the hyperbola is situated be very obtuse, the 
surface itself will very much resemble the ordinary shape of a dice-box. 
Under such an aspect it will be advisable for those who are not familiar 
with the general figure, to first view it ; after which the idea can easily be 
extended to the general case. 

There is another simple aspect under which the genesis of the hyper- 



Digitized by 



Google 



HODflRN OBOMKTBT. 93 

boloid of revolution of one sheet may be viewed. If there be given two 
straight lines which are not in the same plane, invariably connected with 
each other ; and if one of these be made an axis of revolution, the other will 
generate this surface. In this way a model of the surface may be turned 
upon a common lathe, by an oblique position of the cutting edge with 
respect to the axis of motion.* When the one line meets the other, the 
surface becomes the right cone : and when they are parallel, the right 
cylinder. 

XXVIII. 

If three lines be given^ no two of which are in the same plane^ no 
point can he selected in one ofthem^ through which a line cannot he 
drawn to touch the other twoy and in no ease can there he more than 
one such line. 

For through this point and each line one plane can be drawn ; and the 
intersection of these planes will be a line which cuts each of the lines through 
which the planes are drawn. Whence one such line can be always drawn. 

Moreover, as no other planes than these can be drawn through that point 
and the two other lines, and these planes can have but one intersection, there 
can be only one such line drawn through each point of the third line. 

It will, therefore, follow that the line in its motion upon the three direc- 
trices will generate a surface having no double or multiple point, since only 
^ one generatrix can ever pass through the same point. 

It will also follow that the three directrices themselves are situated on the 
surface since they are touched by the generatrices in every position which 
they take. 

It will still further follow that no two positions of the generating line, 
however closely contiguous, are in the same plane. For were that supposi- 
tion admitted, Uie three lines upon which they rested would be in that plane, 
which is contrary to the original conditions, f 

XXIX. 

Let AB, CD, EF he three directrices, no two of which are in the 
same plane : and let AE, BF, GE, he three positions of the generatrix : 
then we shall always have the relation amongst the segments of the 
twisted quadrilateral ABFE, 

AC.DF : CE.BD : : AG.KF : GB.EK. 

Join AF, and produce it if necessary. Then the quadrilateral may be 
viewed as being composed of the triangles ABF, AEF, situated in two 
planes whose intersection is AF. 

* This mode of generating the turface was inTented by Sir Christopher Wren. 

f If two of the directrices be parallel, the h jperbolold is reduced to a plane, generated 
by a line moTing along the third directrix parallel to the two former ones. For the intersec- 
tion of the two supposed planes in the reasoning above, is always parallel to the lines through 
which those planes pass. 

The same is also true when two of the directrices meet in a point; for then the locus is 
the plane which is drawn through that point and the third directrix. 

We may add that the generatrix may take positions parallel to each of the directrices ^-one 
position parallel to each of them ; and that it can take no more than one. For through any 
two of the directrices, planes may be drawn parallel to the third ; and these planes will 
intersect in a generatrix which is parallel to that third directrix, — a well known proposition 
in geometry. Also as only one plane can be drawn through each of the two directrices 
parallel to the third, there can be but one pair of inch planes, and therefore only one such 
generalriz.

XXX. 

The hyperholoid of one sheet tidmits of a second genesis^ — viZy by 
taking any three generatrices AC, GK, BF as directrices^ in which 
case the three original directrices AB, CD, £F will become three posi" 
tions of the new generatrix. 

It is, in the first place clear that the three lines AE, GK, BF being taken 
as directrices, no two of them being in the same plane (xxviii), the lines 
AB, CD, EF will be three positions of the generatrix of a surface of the 
same species as the former one, its genesis being precisely of the same 
kind. It remains to prove that by both modes, the same surface is pro- 
duced. This will be effected if we can shew that every line of the second 
mode of genesis meets every line of the first mode : for then every point in

It follows then from the converse of Hutton prop. zv. (which is too easily

in a point h. 

Wherefore, since G'K' is any line of the first genesis and CD" any line 
of the second, and it has been shown that they v^ meet, the proposition is 
fully established. 

XXXI. 

The surface itself admitting of this double genesis, we see that through 
every point in it, two lines can be drawn which shall lie wholly upon the 
surface, one by each mode of genesis ; and since no two lines of the same 
genesis can meet, there can be only two such lines through any point. 
Also, since every line of one genesis meets every line of the other, the 
surface itself may be imagined to be covered over with a species of net 
work of infinitely small quadrilaterals, the opposite sides of each being in 
different planes, however minute they may be. 

The idea which we thus obtain of the hyperboloid of one sheet does not 
readily assimilate with the idea of a surface of which the hyperboloid of 
revolution of one sheet is a special case, or of a surface all whose plane 
sections are conic sections. On this account it seems necessary to continue 
the investigation to the extent of shewing that however tortuous and 
irregular the surface as resulting from this genesis may appear to the mind, 
it yet possesses regular and determinate features quite as simple in con* 
ception and expression as are possessed by the more familiar class of surfaces 
of the second order.

NOTE ON THE EQUATION OF THE TANGENT TO THE 

PARABOLA. 

The ordinary equation of the tangent to a parabola at a given point 
involves the co-ordinates of that point. It may, however, be put in another 
form involving only the angle which the tangent makes with the axis of ar, 
and in this shape it possesses some advantages, and may be usefully em- 
ployed in the demonstration of several theorems relating to that curve. 

Let a denote the angle which a line cutting the parabola makes with the 
axis of a?; then the equation of the line will be 

y = a?tano + /3 ••.••..•(!) 

and the equation of the parabola is 

2^= Amx (2) 

Treating these as simultaneous equations, we have 
(a?tano+/3)«=i4fwa?, 
or a?»tan* o +2 03 tana — 2«i)a? + /3* = 0. 
Now the roots of this equation are the values of x at the points of inter- 
section of the line with the curve, and if the secant become a tangent, the 
two values of a? must be equal : hence we must have the condition 
4/3»tan» a = 4 (/3 tan a —2m)*.

± /3 tan o = /3 tan a — 2m, or 2m = )3(tan a if: tan a). 

The value of m cannot be zero; therefore the sign — must be rejected, 
and using the sign + we get 

/3 = m cot a. 

Hence the equation of the tangent may be put in the form 

y ^=. SB tan a -)- m cot a (3) 

where a is the angle which the tangent makes with the axis of nc. 

As an instance of the application of this equation, let

drawn from the focus perpendicular to the tangent is 

y = — a? cot a + m cot a (JB) 

Consequently the lines {A) and (^B) intersect in the point whose co- 
ordinates are 0? = 0, y = m cot a ; that is, in the tangent at the vertex.

LVII. Mr. J. W. ElUott, Oreatham. 

If a straight line be drawn from the vertex of any plane triangle to the 
point of contact of either the inscribed or escribed circle ; then the middle 
points of that line and the base, and the centre of the circle, range in the 
same straight line. 

[First Solution. The Rev. John Leech, Curate of May, Tyrone.'] 

Let ABC be a plane triangle, K the middle of the base, O the centre of 
the inscribed circle, OD the radius perpendicular to BC. Draw AO, BO, 
CO, AD, AK, OK, and let AD' be drawn parallel to OK ; draw also 
OD', and produce KO to meet CD in H. 
Then because AD' is parallel to OK, the A. 

triangles AOD' and AD'K are equal ; and if "x, 

to each of these equals the triangle ACD' be / \\ 

added, the quadrilateral ACD'O will be M^ ( 

equal to the triangle CAK, which is one- / \ 

half of the triangle ABO. Again, if perpen- L ' ^ -. .^:::i_&gt;^ 

diculars be drawn from O to the sides AC B D n u ^ 
and AB, it will be evident that the triangles 

AOC and BOD are together equal to one-half of the triangle ABC ; there- 
fore the triangles AOC and BOD are together equal to the quadrilateral 
ACD'O. From each of these equals take the triangle AOC, and the 
remaining triangles COD' and BOD are equal ; therefore the base CD' is 
equal to the base BD, and consequently KD' is equal to KD. Lastly, 
because KOH is parallel to AD', and DD' is bisected in K ; therefore AD 
is also bisected in H. In a similar manner n^ay the property be eatablished 
for any of the escribed circles. 



[Second Solution. Mr. R. Harley, NorthtOich, Cheshire.'] 
Let O, Oi be the respective centres of the inscribed and escribed circles \ 
D, D' their points of contact with the base BC of the triangle ; DP, D'P^ 
their diameters drawn perpendicular to BC ; and K, H, Hi the middle 
points of the lines BC, AD, AD' respectively. 

Then (Modern Geometry, Student, prop. 45, cor. 1 , and prop. 58), 
KD =: KD', and the points A, P, D' are in one straight line. Also tbe 
points A, D, Pi are in one straight line ; therefore the lines KH, KHi are 
respectively parallel to AD', AD ; consequently KH and Hi K produced 
bisect the diameters DP, D'Pi; that is, they pass through the centres 
O, Oi of the inscribed and escribed circles.

ON PROPOSITIONS IN PLANO AND THEIR ANALOGOUS 

PROPERTIES IN THREE DIMENSIONS. 

[Professor Heam, Royal Military CoUege.] 

I propose to discuss the question — Does there always exist a problem in 
three dimensions analogous to a given problem in piano ? 

Before, however, I enter upon this discussion, I would remark in reference 
to a paper in Number 1 , Vol. II, of the Mathematician, by Mr. Fenwick, 
entitled ** A property relative to surfaces which pass through the same 
eight points in space," that the enunciation of the question in the Diary to 
which Mr. Fen wick refers, ought to have been the following: 

" An indefinite number of surfaces of the second order may pass through 
eight points in space. Let diameters be drawn parallel to a given line, 
then will the diametral planes which are conjugate to these diameters all 
pass through the same line." 

I now give my solution to the problem in its amended form. 

Let w = 0, i? = 0, be two equations of the second order in a? and y and 2?, 
representing two of the system of surfaces. Then any of the other surfaces 
will be defined by 

ti 4- ^'V = 0, 
in which X is arbitrary. 

Let-y- = -^ = be equations of the given dfarection, then the 

equation to a plane conjugate to a diameter parallel to this direction is 
du

planes whose equations are

Let us now consider the following j^oblem.

To find the locus of the diameters conjugate to sections which are all 
parallel to a given plane. 

Let Ix + my -{-nz =zO,he the given plane. Then

Under particular conditions the hyperboloid becomes a cone. Mr. 
Fenwick has very ably discussed some of these cases in the paper alluded 
to, but he seems by his note at the end to have considered that he had 
established the non-existence of a theorem in three dimensions analogous 
to that in piano enunciated at the commencement of his paper. The pro- 
blem here given, however, which was that really intended to have been 
proposed in the Diary, is a strict analogy. 

I will now consider the question, *' Does there always exist a problem in 
three dimensions analogical to a given problem in piano ?" 

Every plane problem may, in one sense, be regarded as a solid problem, 
for every straight line may be considered as the intersection of a plane (at 
right angles suppose) to the plane of the diagram, and every curve as the 
Intersection of an orthographic cylinder. Now, instead of such surfaces 
being orthographic, suppose them all to converge to a point at a finite 
distance from the plane of the diagram. Take this point as a centre, and 
describe a sphere cutting the geometrical system, and we have a spherical 
analogy to the proposed plane problem. Generalize one step further. 
Instead of making the surfaces and planes terminate in a point, suppose 
them variously disposed. Still there must exist some law of configuration. 
In other words, since every curve may be considered as composed of an 
infinite number of infinitely small straight lines, and every surface as com- 
posed of an infinite number of planes, the analytical expression of a plane 
problem may be written ^(ffa: + 6y 4" = ^» ^^^^ 's, every plane problem 
requires the combination of a finite or infinite number of equations of the 
form ax -{-by + ] =0. Similarly, the analytical expression of a solid 
problem is T{ax +by -\-cz -^ 1 ) = 0. The former can in every instance 
be generalized into the latter, though the actual performance of the general- 
ization in some instances may involve considerable difficulty. The maxim 



Digitized by 



Google 



PR0FEBTIE8 07 CONIC 8E0TION1. I 19 

* De non apparentibus et non existentibus eadem est ratio,' may do very 
well in law, but certainly not in mathematics. 

Sometimes the generalized problem in three dimensions is self evident, 
and when so, the deduction of the plane problem from it is at Once an easy 
and elegant mode of solution. As an illustration of this remark, I refer 
to Woolhouse's demonstration of the properties of converging triangles, 
(Lady's and Gentleman's Diary, 1842), by a mode of investigation which I 
discovered about the same time as he did. As a further illustration, I here 
present you with a demonstration of the theorem. 

'^ If any two triangles be inscribed in a conic section, they will circum- 
scribe another conic section.*^ 

Since innumerable surfaces of the second order can be made to pass 
through eight points arbitrarily situated in space, or can be made to touch 
eight planes arbitrarily situated, it follows that if any two tetrahedra be 
inscribed in a surface of the second order, their planes will be tangential to 
another surface of the second order. Now, suppose two tetrahedra inscribed 
in a cone of the second order, their planes must touch some surface of the 
second order. Let now one of the angular points of each tetrahedron 
approach to and ultimately coincide with the vertex of the cone. Still the 
eight faces of the tetrahedra are tangential to some surface of the second 
order. Let a cone having the same vertex as that already described 
envelope this surface — then six of the planes of the tetrahedra (namely, 
those through the common vertex of the cones) are necessarily tangential to 
the enveloping cone, and this cone is necessarily one of the second 
order. Now let the geometrical system of two cones and six planes be cut 
by another plane, and the section will be two curves of the second order, 
with two triangles inscribed in the one, also circumscribing the other. 

Instead of cutting the system by a plane, let it be cut by a sphere whose 
centre is the common vertex, and we have a demonstration of the analogical 
^herical problem. 
Royal MiUtary College^ Nov. 20, 1845.

ON THE THEORY OF COLLINEATION. 

iMr. Rutheffard.] 

The collinear theory is analogous to the theory of poles and polars, 
which has heen hoth amply and ably discussed in the pages of the 
Mathematician, In the polar theory, each point of the one system of 
points has a corresponding line of the other system, — in the collinear theory, 
to e^ch point of the one system there is a corresponding /&gt;oin^ of the other 
system, with the condition that if three points of the one system are in a 
straight line, the three corresponding points of the other system are also 
in a straight line ; and therefore to each line in the one system there is a 
corresponding line in the other system. Like the theory of poles and 
polars, the theory of collineation may be extensively applied to a variety 
of researches both in piano and in space, and it will be our object in the 
following discussion to develope the principle in a simple and intelligible 
manner, and then to apply it to some interesting inquiries. 



Definition, — Two figures, or systems of points and lines which are so 
related that to e^ch point of the one system there is a corresponding /&gt;(7in/ 
of the other system, such that if three points of the one system are in a 
straight line, the three corresponding points of the other system are also 
in a straight line, are called collinear figures ^ or collinear systems. 

Thus if the points A, B, C; A\ B', C, are taken so that the three points 
A, B, C are in a straight line, and also the three points A\ B', C in another 
straight line ; and also if six other points a^b^c; a', b\ c' be taken so far 
arbitrarily that a, i, c range in a straight line, and also a\ b\ c' in another 
straight line ; then it is not to be understood that each system of six points 
so taken forms a collinear system, because each system is not limited to six 
or any number of points and lines. If however the points A, 6, G, A', B', C 
and a, 6, c, a\ b\ c belong to two collinear systems, and A, a, B, 6, etc, 
be corresponding points ; then P the point of intersection of the two lines 
A A', BB' in the one system will correspond to p the point of intersection of 
the corresponding lines aa', lib\ etCy in the other system, in conformity 
with the preceding definition. In a similar manner the intersections 
Q, R, S, etc, of the straight lines that can be drawn through pairs of the 
six points A, B, C, A', B', C will correspond to the intersections q^ r, ^, 
etc, of the right lines drawn through the corresponding pairs of the six 
points a, ft, &lt;?, a\ b, c'. The points P, Q, R, etc. which have been intro- 
troduced into the one system, and the points corresponding to themp, q, r, 
etc. which have been introduced into the other system, will in a similar 
manner furnish other corresponding pairs of points, and so on. It is obvious 
that if three of the points thus introduced as P, Q, R in the one system 
range in a straight line, the three corresponding introduced points, /?, ^, r, in 
the other system, must also range in a straight line. That this is not the 
case with points arbitrarily taken is evident, for the first six points may be 
taken such that the points of intersection P, Q, R are in one line, and the 
other six points may be selected in such a manner that the corresponding 
points of intersection p, q, r are not situated in one right line. Such a 
system of points as the latter would not therefore form a system collinear 
with the former, inasmuch as the condition specified in the definition of 
collinear systems is not fulfilled. 



Digitized by 



Google 



124 ON THE THEOBT OF COI.LINKATION. 

Since two points determine a straight line, it is evident that to a straight 
line of the one system there corresponds a straight line of the other system, 
and that a straight line which joins two points of the one system has for its 
corresponding line, that which joins the corresponding points of the other 
system. 

It will be shewn in the next article that, in general, four points of the one 
system, and four corresponding points of the other system are sufficient to 
determine a point of the latter system corresponding to a specified point of 
the former. 

II. 

Let wy be the rectangular or oblique coordinates of a point in the one 
system, and tu those of the corresponding point in the other coUinear system, 
referred to the same or different axes ; then if both systems are given, the 
coordinates tu of the one system will be functions of the coordinates xy of 
the other ; hence if 

gu + ht-^k—O (1) 

be the equation of a line in the system {tu)^ the equation of a line in the 
system {iey\ which is to correspond to (1), will be of the form 

girn'y + «'«+/)+ h(m'y + nx +y) + k[my +nz + 1)=:0. . (2) 

whence if ;p^ be the coordinates oi 9k point in the system {xy)y the coordinates 
of a corresponding j9oin^ in the system {tu) will be 

tt = "''y + "'^ +^' and t = "^'y+^'^+P' (3) 

my+nx+l tny+nx+i ^ ' 

because the substitution of these values for u and ^ in eq. (1) produces the 
corresponding line (2). 

It is obvious, therefore, that if the coordinates of four points of the one

HORNER ON ALGEBRAIC TRANSFORMATION. 

(Concluded from page 37.) 

40. Having thus endeavoured to shew that some important portions of the 
Algebraic and Differential Theories are only exemplifications of a general 
theorem deduced from the principles of algebraic division, I will close these 
remarks by briefly describing how the basis of both theories may, by the 
same simple means, be completed. 

The reader will have remarked that the more usual differential formulae 
are those, whose coefficients occupy diagonal lines in the table of art, 33, 
etc.^ and consequently in the general table in art, 13. The slightest 
variation from the principle [art, 11) on which that table was constructed 
would have produced the formulae in question. It was only requisite to 
stop the division by making/? = m, one term earlier in each course than in 
the next preceding. The effect of this alteration may very easily be intro- 
duced into the formulae in art, 13, by referring to what was shewn in a 
preceding article respecting the cotemporary change effected by division, in 
the unknown variable of one term and the coefficient of the next. For in 
reversing this effect, it is obvious that any number of the latter terms of a 
transformee may be removed into a subsequent one without altering its 
value, if we observe to change the coefficient of the first of the transplanted 
terms for that which belongs to its new situation. 

41. Thus Mq a? + No being changed to Mi a? + N^, this may be substi- 
tuted for the last two terms of the second equation ; which will then end in 
L{a?a?i-|-Miar+No, These terms by merely changing Li for L, may be put 
for the last three terms of the third equation ; and so on. The effect of 
continuing this process through n transformees b obvious, and may be thus 
stated : — 

If we take the terms which descend diagonally from Nq changing Xp for 
a?p.i, they constitute a formula =yv2;. The same applies to the diagonal 
descending from any other absolute. Hence we have

PB0P08ITI0N VII. THEOBEV. 

If from any point in the curw of an ellipse or hyperbola two chords 
be dranm^ and parallel to these respectively two semidiameters ; the 
sector included between the semidiameters is equal to half the sector 
hating for its base the portion of the curve interested by the diverging 
chords. 

From D, any point in the curve of an ellipse, draw two chords DA and 
DB ; and respectively parallel to these draw the semidiameters CE and CF : 
the sector ECF is equal to half the sector ACB. 

For produce EC and FC to meet the curve 
in F, F; then since AD is parallel to EF 
and BD to FF, the sector DCE '= ACE and 
DCF = BCF ; therefore DCE' — DCF = ci 
ACE — BCF ; but DCF— DCF= ECF 
= ECF ; and ACE —BCF = ACB— ECF ; 
hence ECF = ACB — ECF, and ECF = 
ACB 

In the hyperbola the proof is the same, but the chords are supposed to 
terminate in opposite branches.

If through any point in the plane of an ellipse or hyperbola two lines 
be drawny each meeting t?ie curve in two points, and two semidiameters 
be drawn parallel respectively to these lines; the sector included 
between the semidiameters is equal to half the sum or half the difference 
of the sectors having for their bases the portions of the curve intercepted

FEOP. IX. THEOEKH. 

(Corollary to Desargue's Theorem.) 
If a conic section he described about each of the simple quadrilaterals 
of which a complete quadrilateral is composed^ and a transversal be 
drawn intersecting the three curves and the four sides of the quadri- 
lateraly these intersections will^ by Desargue's theorem^ furnish three 
systems of points in involution^ and the centres of any two of these 
systems are conjugate points with respect to the centre of the third 
system.

MATHEMATICAL NOTE. 

[By a Oentleman Cadet] 

The fundamental formula for the sine of the sum of two arcs may be very 
simply deduced in the following manner. 

Let ABC be a plane triangle, and draw CM perpendicular to the side 
AB ; then sin C = sin (A + B). 

Now AB.CM = BM.CM + AM.CM = twice 
triangle ABC, 

and, AC.BC sin C = twice triangle ABC. 

Equating these expressions and dividing by 
AC.BC, gives 

CMBM . AM CM 




or. 



sin ( A+B)= sin A cos B+cos A sin B.

Equations (1), (2), and (2') are of great importance in translating

XXI. 

Four planes being given, it is required to find the locus of a point P, such 
that if PM', PM*" be drawn perpendicular to two opposite planes, and 
PMi, PM, perpendicular to the other two planes; PM'XPM* shall be to 
PMi X PMa in a given ratio.

and therefore the locus is a surface of the second degree passing through

This is analogous to the theorem jld quaiuor lineas of the ancients, 
which probably led Dxscabtes to his general method of applying algebra 
to geometry. 

Join AP, let it meet the surface again in F, and the planes M'N', M*N*,

and consequently the six points P, P', Q, Q', R, R' are in involution. 

This corresponds to the following theorem of Desabques. 

If any quadrilateral be described in a conic section ^ any transversal 
meets the curve and the sides of the quadrilateral in six points which 
are in involution. 

When the three points R, R', Q', and the surface (3) are given, the points 
P and P' become known, and the point Q becomes &amp;ced in consequence of 
the relation (4). 

Hence if four of the intersections of four variable planes move on a 
given surface of tJie second degree^ and if three of the planes pass 
through three given points P, R' and Q', which range upon a straight 
line^ the fourth plane will pass througha fixed point Q, which ranges 
upon the same straight line. 

This is analogous to a well known theorem which appears to be due to 
Bbiahchok. — ^ the Mathematician, Vol. I., p. 289, art. 29.

Hence if two surfaces of the second degree intersect each other along

This is analogous to the following theorem in piano. 

If two conic sections he described about a quadrilateral^ and if any 
transversal cut the curve in four points^ and two opposite sides of the 
quadrilateral in two other points ; these six points are in involution, — 
Stubm, Annal. des Math., tom. xtii. 

When two points P, P' of the locus are given, Q and Q' are fixed points ; 
consequently if one of the planes 9'= 0, be fixed and the other moveable, 
the five points P, P', Q, Q', R', are fixed, and therefore R is also fixed (10) : 
hence, in this case, the moveable plane 9 = will turn about a fixed point R. 
This result was already obtained (11) by a difiTerent process of reasoning. 

When the planes 9 = and 9' = are fixed, if one point P' of the locus 
be given, the locus may be constructed by points. For if any straight line 
be drawn through P' meeting the given surface in Q and Q' and the given 
planes in R and R', the five points Q, Q', R, R', P' are fixed, and the 
position of P may be calculated from (10).

If two parabolas be described on the same axis so that the vertex of the 
second may be the focus of the first, and its parameter half that of the other ; 
then if from the points in which normals of the smaller parabola meet the 
evolute of the larger, as centres, and the respective normals as radii, circles 
he described, these will all pass through a certain fixed point.

intercepted between the evolute and the smaller parabola as radii.
