LXIII. From the Putney College Examination Papers, July, 1846. 
The ground plan and picture being taken as planes of projection, there 
are given two points, and a finite line by means of their projections, and a 
plane by means of its traces. If light emanate from one of the given points, 
and the given line enlightened by it cast a shadow on the given plane, it is 
required :— 

( 1 ) To construct the projections of the shadow on the planes of projection. 

(2) To construct the perspective of that shadow, the eye being in the 
second given point. 

[Solution. Mr, C, H. Brown, Student, C. C, E.] 

The solution is readily effected by means of the two following elementary 
problems, which are given in all works on Descriptive Geometry, and 
which it is sufficient to merely quote as Lemmas, See, for instance, Le- 
fehure de Fourcy, Traiti de GSomitrie Descriptive, probs. i. and iii. 
pp. 44 and 49. 




Lemma 1 . Given the projections of two points in a line, to construct the 
traces of that line. 

Lemma 2. Given the projections of two points in a line, and the traces 
of a plane, to coneti'uct the projections of the intersection of the line and 
plane.

ite traces, PPj and PPj.

^ Pifif Similarly let 717a the projections of the intersection of AC with the 
plane PiPPs be found. Then /3t7i will be the horizontal projection of the 
shadow, and ^37, the vertical. The projections of the shadow are therefore 
found. 

To find the perspective of that shadow, we have only to find where lines 
drawn from its extremities to the eye pierce the vertical plane of projection, 
which in the problem is the picture plane. But we have the horizontal 
projections ^i/3i and ^17, and the vertical projections e^Pz and ^27* ; and hence 
(Lemma 1 ) we find the vertical traces p.^^ of those lines. The line p^^ is 
the perspective required. 

Note, In this sketch the eye is taken on the opposite side of the picture 
plane from the other data, as in perspectioe should be the case. Divested, 
however, of the technical application of the problem, the data may have any 
position whatever : but in such case, the line which represents the perspective 
might not fall within the limits of the picture. 

Good solutions were sent by Messrs. Oodfray and Hearn.

LXV. Mr, Fenwick. 

Prove that the locus of the centres of the innumerable surfaces of the 
second order that hare eight points common in space, is a surface of the 
second order, and thence deduce the corresponding locus in piano. 

The locus in space, it will be seen, is the intersection of two surfaces of 
the second order, that is, two hyperboloids of one sheet (see page 81.) — S.F. 

[Solution. Mr, Thonuu Weddle.] 

LetX = (I) X'=0 (2) be the equations of two of the 

surfaces passing through the eight points, then will 

X + XX' = (3) 

represent all the surfaces, X being arbitrary ; let also 

U = 0, V = 0, W = (4) 

and U'=0, V'=0, W'=0 (5) 

be the equations for determining the centres of (I) and (2) respectively. 
Then will 

U4-XU' = 0, V4-XV' = 0, W+XW' = (6) 

determine the position of the centre of (3), and the elimination of the 
variable X will give the locus of the centre. The elimination being effected, 
we have 

UV — U'V = 0, UW — U'W = 0, VW — V'W = 0....(7) 

and any one of the equations (7) is a consequence of the other two. We 
hence infer that in general the locus is a curve which is the intersection of 
two surfaces of the second order. 

The preceding investigation will apply to curves of the second order 
passing through four points in a plane, if (1, 2) represent two of the 
curves, and those equations be rejected which contain W or W. We shall 
find finally, UV — U'V = 0, from which we see that the locus is a curve 
of the second order. 

We may observe that the locus would be a surface of the third order, if 
the surfaces were restricted to pass through seven points only'. 

It may not perhaps be much out of place to notice the following theorem 
here, as it is somewhat connected with the above, as well as with various 
discussions in the *' Mathematician.** 

Let diameters conjugate to a given plane be drawn to the innumerable 
surfaces of the second order passing through eight points in space^ the 
locus qf these diameters is a surface of the second order. 

Let U = 0, V = 0, and U' = 0, V = 0, be the equations to the diameters 
of (1) and (2) respectively, which are conjugate to the given plane; then 
will the diameter of (3) conjugate to the given plane be denoted by 

U+XU' = 0, V4-XV' = 0. 

The elimination of X gives U V — U'V = 0, for the locus which is there- 
fore a surface of the second order.

LXIX. Mr. J. W. Elliott, Greatham. 

If from a point P in a conic section, a tangent PO be drawn to meet the 
axis major AB in O, and the chords AP, BP to meet a perpendicular 
through O in Q, Q' : then QO = OQ'. 

[First Solution. Mr, H. Godfray, David Place, St. Mark's Road, Jersey ; 
and similarly by Mr. Thomas Weddle, Netocastle.] 

This theorem admits of greater generality than as proposed in the 
exercise ; it may be thus enunciated : — If from a point P in a conic section, 
a tangent PO be drawn to meet any diameter AB in O, and the chords 
AP, BP to meet a conjugate to AB in Q, Q' ; then QO = Q'O. 

For draw the ordinate PM, then (Davies's Hutton, II, page 138, eor. 1, 
for the ellipse, and page 164, cor, I, for tlie hyperbola) AB is divided 
harmonically in M and O : 

.-. AM : AO : : BM : BO. 
But by similar triangles, 

AM : AO : : PM : QO, and BM : BO :: PM : Q'O ; 
.-. QO = Q'0. 
The same property holds for the parabola by considering the chord 
through A to be another diameter through P. 

The solution of Mr. Samuel Bills, Hatvton, was analogous to the 
preceding, AB being the major axis, as in the exercise.

LXXII. Mr. Rutherford. 

If three equal forces be represented hi magnitude and direction by the 
three lines drawn from the angular points of a plane triangle to the centre 
of the circumscribed circle, their resultant will be represented in magnitude 
and direction by the straight line which joins the centre of the circumscribed 
circle and the point of intersection of the perpendiculars from the angles on 
the opposite sides. 

[First Solution. William Burns, L.L.D., Rochester.] 

Let ABC be the triangle, O the centre of the circumscribed circle, P the 
point of intersection of the porpendiculars AD, BN ; and draw OA, OB, 
OC, OP. Draw also Oil, OK perpeiulicular to BC, CA, and join HK. 
Since AK = KC, and CH = HB, therefore HK is 
parallel to AB, and AB equal to 211K. Also the tri- 
angles APB and HOK being equiangular, BP = 20K. 
Hence if the force OC be resolved into forces represented 
in magnitude and direction by OK, KC, and OA into two 
represented by OK, KA ; then KC, KA being equal and 
opposite, 20K = BP is the resultant of O A, OC. Also 
if OB be resolved into the two OP, PB, then since BP 
and PB are equal and opposite, OP is evidently the direction and magnitude 
of the resultant of the three forces OA, OB, OC.

SOLUTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL EXERCISES. 169 

[Second Solution. Mr. Thomas Weddle ; and similarly by Messrs. S.Bills, 
and W. H, Levy^ Shalboume. ] 
Draw OH and AD perpendicular to BC : on AD take AP = 20H, and 
draw OP ; then P is the point of intersection of the perpendiculars from 
A, B, C on the opposite sides (Mathematical Companion, No. V, page 48). 
Now the force OA is equivalent to the forces PO and PA ; OC to OH and 
HC ; and OB to OH and HB ; hence the three forces OA, OB, OC are 
equivalent to the forces PO, PA, 20H, HC and HB acting at O. Of these 
PA and 20H are equal and opposite, and so are HC and HB ; hence the 
forces OA, OB, OC are equivalent to the single force OP, that is, the force 
OP is the resultant of the three equal forces OA, OB, OC. 

[Third Solution. Mr. J. W. Elliott, Greathnm, Stockton-on-Tees; and 

similarly by Messrs. J. Reid, Prior's Salford; H. Godfray, Jersey ; 

B. Barley, Northvnch; and H.B.L,^ 

Let ABC he a plane triangle, O the centre of its circumscribed circle, and 
AO, BO, CO the lines representing the three equal forces in magnitude and 
direction. Draw OHR perpendicular to BC, and make OR = 20H ; then 
OR represents in magnitude and direction the resultant of BO, CO, as is 
well known. 

Again, complete the parallelogram AORP, and draw the diagonal OP, 
which will represent in magnitude and direction the resultant of the forces 
OA, OR, that is, of the three forces AO, BO, CO. 

It now only remains to show that P is the point of intersection of perpen- 
diculars from A, B, C upon the opposite sides. 

Since CO = OB, OR bisects the angle COB and intersects BC at right- 
angles in H; and therefore AP being parallel to OR by construction, 
coincides with a perpendicular from A upon BC. Moreover AP=OR 
=:20H ; hence P is the point of intersection of perpendiculars from A,B,C. 
Vide Lady*s and Gentleman's Diary, for 1843, p. 60. 

[Fourth Solution. By Dunelmensis.] 
Let A be the vertex of the plane triangle ; BC the base ; O the centre of 
the circumscribed circle, and R its radius. Then resolving the forces 
parallel and perpendicular to the base BC, we have for the component 
forces of 

(OA) R sin(B — C) and R cos(B-- C), 

(OB) . . . . R cos(90°— A) and — R 6in(90°— A), 
(OC). .— R cos(90^— A) and — R sin(90O— A) ; 
.-. 2(X) = Rsin(B-C) } 

2(Y) = Rcos(B — C)— 2RcosAS ^ ' 

Again, since AP = 20H, the component forces of OP are evidently, 
R sin(B — C) , and R co8(B — C) — 2R cos A, 
which are identical with 2(X) and 2(Y) by (I); hence the resultant is 
represented, both in magnitude and direction, by the line OP. 

Messrs. Heara and Wright also sent good solalions of this exercise.

80. By Juvenis. 

A merchant bought a cask of spirits for £48, and sold a quantity exceeding 
three-fourths of the whole by two gallons at a profit of £25 per cent. He 
afterwards sold the remainder at such a price as to clear £60 per cent by the 
whole transaction ; and had he sold the whole quantity at the latter price he 
would have gained £175 percent: find the number of gallons contained 
in the cask. 

J^ote, This exercise is to be solved by the principles of arithmetic. 
81 ^ Mr. B, HarUy^ J^orthwich. 

From each angle of a quadrilateral ABCD inscribed in a circle let a line 
be inflected to the opposite diagonal, so that it and the other diagonal may 
make equal angles with the adjacent sides : then will the areas of the 
triangles ABC, ADC, ABD, BCD, into which the quadrilateral is divided 
by its diagonals, be to each other respectively as the four inflected lines. 

82. By Geomeiricus. 
Given the sum of the two sides of a plane triangle, the radius of the 
inscribed circle, and either of the angles at the base, to construct the triangle. 

83. Mr. James Beidj Prior's Salford. 
If from the point in which a normal to an ellipse meets the axis major a 
straight line be drawn at right angles to the normal, the part of either of the 
focal distances intercepted between this line and the curve will be an 
harmonic mean between the focal distances.

The equation in general of a surface of the second order when referred to 
any three coordinate planes, involves nine independent coefficients ; if, how- 
ever, the planes of reference be tangential to the surface, three of the nine 
coefficients will be functions of the remaining six. It is required to express 
the equation of the surface in terms of the six coefficients.

The eollinear axis and the eollinear centre are determined geometrically 
in the following manner. 

Let ABCD and A'B'C'D' be two eollinear and collinearly situated 




quadrilaterals : produce A6 and A'B' and let them intersect in the poifit P : 
also produce AD and A'ly and let them intersect in the point Q : draw the 
line PQ, then PQ is the eollinear axis. Also draw the lines AAS BB' 
through the corresponding points A, A' and B, B', and let them meet in O ; 
then Uie point of intersection O is the eollinear centre. 

From what has been shown in (v), it is evident that each pair of corre- 
sponding sides of the eollinear quadrilaterals will, when produced if neces- 
sary, intersect in the eollinear axis PQ, and also that the lines drawn through 
each pair of corresponding points will pass through 0, the eollinear centre.

(1.) IfBiraighi lines be drawn from the angles of a triangle through 

avf/ point in the plane of the triangle to meet the sides ^ and the lines 

Joining these points of intersection and the sides of the triangle he

straight line. 

Let ABC be the triangle (see last figure), and O the point in its plane 
through which and the angular points of the triangle, the lines AOA', BOB', 
COC are drawn. Also draw AB', B'C, C'A' and let them be produced 
and meet the sides AB, BC, AC in P, R, S; then will the points P, R, S 
be in a straight line. 

For the point O can be regarded as the collinear centre, and A', B', C as 
points corresponding respectively to the points A, B, C : therefore AB, A'B'; 
BC, B'C; AC, A'C are corresponding lines ; and since (y) corresponding 
lines intersect in the collinear axis, the three points of intersection P, R, S 
must be situated in the collinear axis PRS, and therefore they are in a 
straight line.

one diagonal of each quadrilateral), will pass through the same point.

corresponding lines BD, B'D' must intersect in the collinear axis B'D" ; 
therefore the diagonals AC, A'C, B'D" tend to the same point F in the 
collinear axis B'D*. Again since B, B' and D, D' are corresponding points,

We have therefore determined four new points in which three straight 
lines constantly intersect, and therefore these points may be taken as collinear 
centres, and we may determine other lines which intersect three and three 
in one point. 

Hence from the last theorem it follows that if the three collinear centres 
of three collinear figures ^ which are two and troo collinearly situated^ 
are in the same straight line^ the three collinear axes intersect in the 
same point. 

(3.) Three points are situated in a straight line, and lines are drawn 
from these points to any arbitrary point : from two of the given points 
draw lines to any point in the line passing through the arbitrary point 
and the third given pointy intersecting the two lines drawn through the 
arbitrary point and the two specified points ; then the line drawn 
through the two points of intersection will always pass through a fixed 
point in the straight line in which the three given points are situated. 

Let A, A', A' be three points in a straight 
line, and P any arbitrary point ; draw the lines 
PA, PA', PA', and let Q be any point in the 
line PA". Let the lines drawn through Q 
and the two points A, A' intersect PA and 
PA' in B and B' ; then the line joining B, B' 
will pass through a fixed point O in the line 
AAA*. 

For A, A' and B, B' can be regarded as 
corresponding points of two systems, and the 
point of intersection (O) of the lines joining 
these corresponding points as the collinear 
centre. Now since A and A' are on opposite 
sides of the point O, the principle in (vii) will " 
here be applicable ; therefore AB', A'B are corresponding lines as well as 
AB, A'B', and they must therefore intersect in the collinear axis ; PQA' is 
consequently the collinear axis. Take any point G in PA and draw 00 
intersecting PA' in C ; then and C are corresponding points, and hence 
AO' and A'O are corresponding lines ; these lines must then intersect in a 
point Q' in the collinear axis, and therefore, conversely, if from any arbitrary 
point Q in the line PA'' two lines be drawn through A and A', meeting PA 
in B and PA' in B', the line joining the points of intersection B and B will 
always pass through a fixed point in AA', which is the collinear centre of 
the two systems. 

In this manner a variety of propositions may be readily demonstrated, and 
it will be seen that the collinear theory applies very happily to a large class 
of interestmg and elegant theorems.

Hence if h and h! have like signs, both curves will be intersected or not 
intersected by the straight line drawn through the origin, and if they have 
different signs, the line will only cut one of the curves. In other words 

— is positive in the first two positions of the curves, and negative in the 

latter position. Consequently the values of p^ m, n are real in the former 
cases and imaginary in the latter; and therefore it is obvious that two 
curves of the second degree which touch two given straight lines can always 
be regarded as coUinear and coUinearly situated figures, if both curves are 
on the same side of the origin, or one of the curves on one side of the origin,

and the other on the other or vertically opposite side. The point of inter-

point of view, because the quantities yjh and yjK may be either positive or 
negative, and hence two vsdues can be assigned to each of the quantities 
/?, wt, «•

equations, we get

which are the polars of the origin or collinear centre, and consequently the 
chords of contact of the axes of coordinates with both curves. 

Hence it follows that both collinear axes intersect in these chords of 
contact, and therefore the point of intersection of the collinear axes coincides 
with the point of intersection of the chords of contact. 

If one of the curves cuts one or both collinear axes, the other curve 
also cuts one or both collinear axes in tlie same points. 

For to a point in the collinear axis of one system, there is a corresponding 
point in the collinear axis of the other system, and if both systems are col- 
linearly situated, these points are coincident. This may be shown also in 
the following way.

In a similar manner it may be shown that two confocal curves of the 
second degree can be regarded as collinear and coUinearly situated figures, 
and the common focus as the collinear centre.

XXXII. 

Upon any three lines in spaccy no two of which are in the same 
plane f nor all three parallel to the same plane, a parallelopiped may 
he constituted having these lines as three non-contiguous and non- 
parallel edges ; and there can be only one such parallelopiped con- 
stituted on the same three lines* 
LetAB',BC',

given lines, un-

three parallel to

Through AB'

A B'. These 

planes, also, as

are parallel to

The three 

pairs of parallel 

planes thus con-

their mutual in- 
tersections form a parallelopiped, having AB', BC, CA' for non-contiguous 

and non-parallel edges. 

One such parallelopiped can therefore be always constituted on the three 
lines, as stated in the proposition. Also, since the pairs of parallel planes 
through the lines AB', BC, CA' are unique, the parallelopiped formed by 
them is, of necessity, unique.

Draw the diagonals AA', BB', CC, DD' of the parallelopiped : these, as 
is known, all meet in a point, P, and bisect each other there. Then, of the 
parallel edges to the three given lines, those which join the extremities of 
the same diagonals (DD' being, for the present excluded) have this pro- 
perty i— of each resting upon two of the directrices of tJie curve surface 
generated by a line moving along them, and being parallel to the third 
directrix. Thus, B'C rests upon AB' and CA' and is parallel to BC ; 
C'A rests upon A B' and BC and is parallel to CA' ; A'B rests upon BC 
and C A' and is parallel to AB'. These are the three lines referred to in the 
foot note to art. xxviii : and they have received the name of symmetrical 
edges : so that AB', BA' are symmetrical to one another, as are likewise 
BC' and C'A symmetrical respectively to B'C and CA'. 

The six edges in question form a twisted hexagon, (hexagon gatiche) 
whose opposite sides are respectively equal and parallel. The alternate 
Bides are successively a directrix and a generatrix ; and these are inter- 
changeable in virtue of art. xxx.

XXXV. 

Since A'B rests upon the two directrices A'C, BC and is parallel to the 
third AB' it is one of the generatrices meeting the third directrix at an 
infinite distance. It is therefore situated upon the hyperbolo'id. In the 
same manner, B'C and C'A are also generatrices of the hyperbolo'id : and 
they form an unique system. 

Now any three of the generatrices may be taken as directrices, as has been 
shewn in (xxx), and hence if we make the interchange in the case before 
us, AB', B'C, CA will become the three corresponding generatrices, forming 
also the corresponding unique system. 

It, therefore, follows that for every generatrix of the first mode, abe^ there 
is one of the second mode, a'b'c\ parallel to it and symmetrically situated. 
From this we see that the surface is altogether symmetrical with respect to 
the parallelepiped formed upon its three defining directrices ts non«conti- 
guous and non-parallel edges ; and that the surface has a centre, P, with 
respect to which it is perfectly symmetrical. 

Again, since for every line abe of the first genesis there is another aVc 
parallel to it in the second, the plane diametral sections along any one of 
the generatrices, drawn through the centre, is composed of two indefinitely 
extended parallel lines, equidistant from the centre. 

Moreover, if any three new directrices be taken, which are in positions 
consistent with the original ones (that is, which would have given the 
surfaces, or which is the same thing, would be generatrices of the second 
system founded on the first), the point P will remain unaltered, though the 
angular points and the edges of the resulting parallelepiped will he altered 
by this change. For the six edges will he symmetrical and parallel in the 
same manner that the two corresponding ones abc^ a'b'c' were proved to be. 

XXXYI. 

Instead of the parallelopiped on DD' let us conceive that another is con- 
stituted on dd^ Uiese points being on DD' produced or not, and equidistant 
from P, and the planes through d^ c^' parallel to those through D, D' 
respectively: then this new parallelopiped will be similar and similarly 
situated to the original one, and the six edges which constitute the new 
twisted hexagon will be parallel to the corresponding ones of the original 
hexagon, and similarly situated with respect to the diagonal. 

The triangles in which the planes through d and d' meet the cutting plane 
will have their sides parallel to those of afiy, «'/8Y respectively, and will 
have/? for their pole. They will also approach or recede from oBy, a'p'y by

equal quantities estimated on ap^ fip, 7^ respectively ; and bence the entire 
figure composed from these triangles will be similar and similarly situated 
with respect to op, /3p, 7/?, whatever be the length of dd'. Whence the 
conic sections which pass through the six intersections of the edges of the 
variable twisted hexagon are always similar and similarly situated with 
respect to pa, pft, p^. 

Again, from very similar reasoning it follows that if the original twisted 
hexagon be cut by any plane parallel to that which contains 0)87, the conic 
section through the intersections of that plane with the six edges will always 
be similar and similarly situated with respect to the pole and its corresponding 
radiants. 

XXXYII. 

The preceding properties are intended to shew that there are several aspects 
under which the surface, distorted as it at first appears, becomes possessed 
of regular features. Before proceeding to shew that all the generatrices 
intersect the plane in the conic section already determined, it may be worth 
while to add one other important property indicative of the regularity of the 
surface. 

Take any one of the twisted quadrilaterals into which the surface is 
divided by t?ie directrices and generatrices .- the line which bisects its 
diagonals always passes through the centre, P, of the parallelopiped 
upon DD'. 

Let FGHK be the quadrilateral, 
whose diagonals are bisected in M 
and N respectively : it is to be 
shewn that MN will pass through 
the centre, P, of the parallelopiped 
described on three directrices of the 
hyperboloid of one sheet, of which 
either FG, KH or KF, OH are 
two. 

Through FG, KH draw the paral- 
lel planes RV, ST ; and through 
KF, GH the parallel planes RS, 
VT. These pairs of planes are, as 
is well known, unique. 

Now the lines FH, GK which 
form the diagonals, (lying between the parallel planes RS, VT, and being 
likewise bisected in M and N), the points M,N are situated in a plane tu 
equidistant from RS, VT and parallel to them. 

In the same way it is shewn that the points M, N are situated in a plane 
rv equidistant from RV, ST and parallel to them. 

Whence, being in two planes which are respectively parallel to the two 
pairs of planes, they are in the intersection of ut, rv. 

But RV, ST are two faces of the parallelopiped depending on FG, KH as 
directrices in one mode of genesis, and hence the plane rv passes through the 
centre P of that parallelopiped. In the same manner KF, GH bebg taken 
as two of the directrices of the second system, the plane tu passes through 
the centre of the corresponding parallelopiped, which is the same with the 
former one P. Both planes, therefore, pass through P ; and hence their 
intersection MN also passes through P, as affirmed in the enunciation.

LXXIX. Professor Hearn^ R, M. College^ Sandhurst. 

On a rough board inclined at a given angle to the horizon a rough ball 
is projected horizontally : required its path and a confirmation of the result 
from actual experiment. 

[Solution. Mr. Heam, the Proposer.] 

The following principles are assumed as the basis of calculation. 

1st. That a ball may describe any path by rolling on a plane, provided 
the axis of instantaneous rotation passes through the centre of curvature of 
the path at every instant. 

2nd. That when the dimensions of the ball are small compared with the 
radius of curvature of the path, the axis of instantaneous rotation may with- 
out sensible error be regarded as parallel to the plane and at right angles to 
the path.

Hence we conclude as follows, that the actual path has an osculation of the 
third order with the parabolic path described, ceteris paribus, on a smooth 
plane, and seneibly coincides with it for a considerable extent, but that 
afterwards the horizontal range in the path mth friction falls sensibly short 
of the range in the path without friction. But although the form of a 
part of the path described is sensibly the same in both cases, the velocity 
vrith which it is described will be sensibly different. Thus on reference to 
(6) we see that gain of vis viva of motion of translation without friction is to 
its gam with friction as I -)-/* : 1 or 7 : 5. 

All these conclusions I have fully verified by experiment, and my object in 
proposing the problem was to call the attention of students to a mode of 
investigation in cases of the kind pursued by Mr. Eamshaw and subsequently 
by Mr. Walton. 

See Eamshaw's Dynamics, p. 280, 3d edition, and Walton's Mechanical 
Problems, p. 444. The problem proposed in those works is as follows :~* 
A circular board has a motion of rotation round a vertical axis passing through 
its centre perpendicular to its plane, it moves with uniform angular velocity, 
and a ball is laid upon it ; supposing friction to prevent sliding, it is required 
to shew that the path of the ball In free space is a circle concejitric mth 
the centre of the hoard. igitized by Google 



218 SOLUTIONS OF MATHEJf A.TICAL EXEBCISES. 

This has always seemed to me to be a strange result, and quite at variance 
&gt;vith experience ; but it will be seen that the authors to whom I refer 
produce a still stranger result; namely, that witli certain initial conditions 
the path may be a circle noi concentric with the centre of the board. 

I will here give my opinion concerning their mode of investigation. 

The friction is resolved in the directions of x and y and taken proportional to 
(fw , (f'l/ 



"-■^m'^m^m'^ii)' 



This mode of investigation thefiefore comes to this, — That the friction acts 
ohliquely to the path, and may be resolved into fi -y-j- tangential to the path, 

and fi perpendicular to it, v being the velocity, and p the radius of cur- 

P 
vature. But friction and every other resfeting force always acts contrary to 
the motion, and therefore tangentially to the path described. It seems 
therefore to be explained how these authors have come to such strange con- 
clusions. If their method be applied to the problem here proposed, the 
equations of motion are 

and the path described is the parabola whose equation is 

a^ = 4(^ + 1) H cosec t.y, 
which makes the horizontal range with friction greater than that without 
it, in the ratio of \/l+tt : 1 or v^7 : ^5. I am satisfied that this is not 
borne out by experiment. 

I will merely add, that I have treated Mr. Earnshaw's problem by the 
method pursued in this paper, and have found in genersd a spiral path 
receding from the centre of motion, and that for certain initial conditions both 
the relative and absolute paths are logarithmic spirals. 



LXXX. By Juvenia. 

A merchant bought a cask of spirits for £48, and sold a quantity exceeding 
three-fourths of the whole by two gallons at a profit of £25 per cent. He 
afterwards sold the remamder at such a price as to clear £60 per cent by the 
whole transaction ; and had he sold the whole quantity at the latter price he 
would have gained £175 per cent: find the number of gallons contained 
in the cask. 

[First Solution. Henri Mandeux, the Shepherd qfTaunane,] 

•.• This wonderftil French youth barin; lately Tisited Jersey to exhibit his extraordinary powers of 
mental calculation, the above exercise was proposed to him, and a nearly instantaneous answer was 
iriven. At the request of our Talued correspondent, Mr. Godfrey, he wrote out his solution, of which 
the foUowinsr translaUon has been sent us by that gentleman. 

He sells the first portion at a profit of £25 per cent, and the last at £ 175 
per cent, and gains £60 per cent on the whole. The first profit is less than 
the mean profit by £35 per cent, and the second is greater by £115 per 
cent ; he has therefore sold 115 parts of the first against 35 of the second ;

If froni the point in which a normal to an ellipse meets the axis major a

[First Solution. Dr. Burns, Rochester; and Mr, Thomas Weddlr,

FP : FQ :: FP : F'Q' ; 
or since PQ = pQ' 

F'P : FP :: FP — PQ : PQ — FP. 
That is, PQ or PQ' is a harmonic mean between FP and FP.

LXXXVI. By Amicus. 



In every tetrahedron, the sum of the squares of the six edges is equal to 
four times the sum of the squares of the lines which join the middles of the 
opposite edges. 

[First Solution. Mr. J. W. Elliott, GreathamJ] 

Let ABCD be the tetrahedron, and O, H, K, L, M, N, the middle points 
of the edges AC, AB, BD, BC, DA, DC respectively. 

Then, since the line drawn to bisect two sides of a triangle is parallel to 
the third side, and equal to half of it, we have 

BC = 2KN = 2HG, DC = 2MG = 2LK, 

AC = 2HL = 2MN, DA = 2KH = 2GN, 

AB=:2LG = 2KM, BD = 2HM = 2LN. 

Now since EN and HG are parallel to BC they are parallel to one another 
(Etie. xi, 9) ; and each being the half of BC, they are also equal to one 
another. Wherefore KNGH is a parallelogram, the diagonals of which are 
GK, HN ; and hence by a known property, 

2GK» + 2HN« = 4HK' + 4KN^ = AD' + BC«. 

Taking in the same way the parallelograms MNHL and MKGL, and 
writing Uie entire results, we have 

2GK'» 4- 2HN' = AD' + BC», 

2HN* + 2ML« = BD' + AC, 

2ML' + 2GK» = AB' + DC. 
These being added give the result stated in the theorem. 
Corollary. The three diagonals GK, HN, LM meet in one point O. 
This is easily proved to be the centre of gravity of the tetrahedron.

LXXXVII. Mr. Thomas JTeddle. 
An ellipse can be divided geometrically into n equal sectors when n is such,that 
a regular polygon of w sides can be described geometrically; required proof. 
[Solution. Mr. O. W. Heam.'\ 
Let it be required to divide a given ellipse into n equal sectors by lines 
drawn from its centre, when n is such that a regular polygon of n sides can 
be described. 
Let a and b be the semi-axes of the ellipse, and suppose a circle with 

radius h described whose plane is inclined at an angle cos "^^— to the plane 

of the ellipse, and so that the axis minor of the ellipse may be parallel to the 
plane of the circle, and the line joining the centres of the circle and ellipse 
at nght angles to the plane of the former. Then the circle will be the 
orthographic projection of the ellipse, and any area in the plane of the ellipse 
projected on the plane of the circle will have to its projection the ratio h : a. 
Hence if the ellipse be divided into equal sectors their projections will be 
equal sectors of tlie circle, and conversely, if a regular polygon be described 

Digitized by VjOOQ IC 



MATHEMATICAL EXEKCI8ES. 227 

in the circle, and lines drawn from its angular points perpendicular to the 
plane of circle, these lines will intersect l he ellipse in points which being 
joined with its centre will divide it into equal sectors. 

We may now conceive the circle and ellipse to be concentric, and a 
diameter of the circle consequently coincident with the axis minor of the 
ellipse, tlien, as in Descriptive Geometry, the sides of the orthographic 
cylinder will ultimately become straight lines parallel to the axis major of 
the ellipse. 

Hence the following construction :^ 

On the axis minor of the ellipse describe a circle. Inscribe in it a regular 
polygon of w sides, and through its angular points draw lines parallel to the 
axis major of the ellipse, and join their points of intersection with the ellipse, 
and the centre, the ellipse will thus be divided into n equal sectors. 

From similar considerations, if a circle be described on the major axis, and 
lines be drawn parallel to the minor axis through the angular points of the 
inscribed polygon, the same thing may be effected. 

Also, since we may choose the first angle of the polygon inscribed in the 
circle arbitrarily, it follows not only that the ellipse may be geometrically 
divided into n equal sectors, but this in an infinite variety of ways. 

Also that an ellipse may (in an infinite number of ways) be divided into 
sectoreal areas which shall have given ratios, provided a circle can be so 
divided.

ON THE GREATEST INSCRIBED, AND LEAST CIRCUM- 
SCRIBED ELLIPSE, TO A GIVEN

[Mr. Fenwick,'] 

The second of the following propositions was discussed by the celebrated 
EuLEB. Owing, however, to his peculiar mode of investigation, Euleb's 
solution is very long and complicated. See the JsTova jleta ^ecuL. 
Petropol vol IX., p. 132. 

I am not aware that the problem, '^ To find the greatest inscribed ellipse 
to a given quadrilateral figure,'* has hitherto engaged the attention of any 
writer, though it leads to a discussion even more interesting than the other. 
Its investigation, it will be seen, results in a quadratic equation, one root 
of which gives the greatest inscribed ellipse, die other, an imaginary area. 
I have endeavoured to account for the origin of this imaginary expression.

Scholium 1. The expression (21), as we have seen, does not fulfil the 
conditions of the problem, inasmuch as it gives an imaginary expression for 
the area of the inscribed ellipse. It evidently belongs to the hyperbola of 
which ac — 6' is a maximum, and to which the sides of the given quadrila- 
teral are tangential. We might indeed have anticipated such a result from 
the fundamental equation (5) which represents central curves in general. 

We may now advert for a little to the line (19) which is the locus of the 
centres of all conic sections which touch the sides or the sides produced, of 
the given quadrilateral, PQRS. 

The coordinates of the middle points of the diagonals PR, QS, are 
evidently 

and as these satisfy (Id), it follows that 

u4ll conic sections that touch the sides or the sides produced of a 
given quadrilateral^ have their centres situated on the straight line 
rohich bisects the diagonals of the quadrilateral. 

This property has been noticed in respect of the ellipse. The hyperbolas 
obviously touch QR, RS ; and PQ, PS produced.

To determine the relations between the points in which the three surfaces 
are cut by an arbitrary transversal, let a straight line be drawn from the

the surface c =0 in Ri, R,, R,. . . . R^ , and the surface (2) in Pj.Pj. •.-?,;

i»+»+2 points in involution. 

Hence if a surface of the n^ degree pass through the intersection of 
two given surfaces not exceeding the n^degreCy and if any transversal 
he drawn meeting the three surfaces^ the points in which it cuts any 
two of the surfaces and any two of the points in which it cuts the third 
surface^ are in involution. 

Since the poinU P, Q, R, taken two at a time, give J n{n—l), i«(«-.l), 
lm(m — 1) combinations respectively; the transversal contains n[n — 1) 
systems of w4-n + 2 points in involution, and ^m{m — 1) systems of 
2n -|- 2 points in involution. 

This proposition is analogous to the theorem of Sturm relative to a system 
of three conic sections passing through the same four points. We shall see 
hereafter that it is true when one or both of the surfaces are complex.

NOTE ON PRATT'S DEMONSTRATION OF THE PARALLELO- 

GRAM OF FORCES. 

[Mr. J. P. Heaiker, A,M,f Royal MUUory Academy,'] 

Pratt in his ^* Mechanical Philosophy" attempts to give a completely 
analytical proof of the principle of the parallelogram of forces, and founds 
it upon an assumption, which he expresses in the following terms : — 
*^ That a force can produce no effect in a direction perpendicular to its own 
direction." Now I think I shall he ahle to show that this expression must 
either have no meaning at all, or that it must be taken in the same sense as 
though we should say, '^ a force can produce no effect in any direction what- 
ever except its own." For if it be meant to say that a force cannot be 
resolved into two others, the direction of one of which shall be at right angles 
to its own direction, the assertion would be manifestly incorrect ; but, if it 
be only meant to say that a number of forces, having their directions all 
lying along two straight lines at right angles to each other, must have the 
algebraic sum of the forces acting along the one of these lines, and also the 
sum of those acting along the other separately equal to zero, in order that 
the whole may be in equilibrium, then the proposition is equally true of a 
number of forces having their directions all lying along any two straight 
lines, inclined at any angle whatever. This is evident from the equation 
R' = P + Q^ 4- 2PQ cos a : and also follows immediately, from the con- 
sideration, that any two forces acting upon a point, in directions not along 
the same straight lines must have a finite resultant ; for if the forces acting 
along any two lines are not separately in equilibrium, they all resolve into 
two forces, which not acting in opposite directions, along the same straight 
line, cannot counteract each other's effects. The values oi/(fi) for the par- 
ticular values of set down by Mr. Pratt, are nevertheless true, because in 
each of these particular cases, one of the two directions into which the power 
P is resolved, is in the same line with its own direction, in which case the 
component along the other direction vanishes, whatever be the angle which 
this direction makes with the direction of P. 



PRACTICAL METHOD OF FORMING LOGARITHMS AND 

ANTI.LOGARITHMS, INDEPENDENTLY OF 

EXTENSIVE TABLES. 

[Professor Heam, Boyal Military College,'] 

The accompanying concise tables are intended for obtaining the logarithms 
of numbers consisting of ten or less than ten digits to ten places of the 
mantissa of the logarithm. If fewer places be required the tables are of 
course equally available, and the indispensable calculation considerably 
curtailed. Again, if logs, to 20 figures were required, tables for finding 
them might easily be framed on the same principle, and they would be but 
double the extent of those here presented. 

In the *' Anti-Logarithmic" set. Table t. contdns the logarithms of the 
nine digits; ii. contains the logarithms of 11, 12 etc, to 19 inclusive; iir. 
contains those of 101, 102, 103 etc. to 109 inclusive ; it. contains those of 
1001, 1002, 1003 etc. to 1009 inclusive, and so on. 

In the " Logarithmic" set, Table i. contains the arithmetical complements 
of the logarithms of 10 — 1, 10 — 2, 10 — 3 etc, to 10 — 9, or of 9, 8, 7, 
etc, to 1; II. contains the ar. comp. log. of 100-^1, 100 — 2 etc, to 
100 — 9, or 99, 98, 97 etc. to 91 ; iii. those of 1000— 1, 1000 — 2 etc. 
to 1000 — 9, or 999, 998 ^f^. to 991 , and so on. 

After this brief but suflicient description we will take at random ten

Let a^z^ x'f/z'j ete.y represent the angular points of tlie polyhedron; 
then if the figure be resolved into triangular pyramids by planes drawn Grom

8. Scholium.*^ince each of the triangular pyramids of which the poly- 
hedron is composed is supposed to be homogeneous, the weights 99, 9V , etc. 
are proportional to the volumes of the pyramids ; and these may readily be 
expressed in terms of the coordinates zyzj z'y'i^^ etc. Hence equations 
(6) determine the coordinates of the centre of gravity of the polyhedron as 
functions of the coordinates of its kngular points. It is evident also that (2) 
and (4). determine tiie coordinates of the centre of gravity of the perimeter 
and surface of a polygon, as functions of the coordinates of its angular 
points; since the quantities n?, 72?', ^^., are proportional to the lengdis^of 
the sides of the polygon in the former case ; and to the areas of the triangles 
of which it is composed in the latter ; and both of these are easily expressed 
in terms of those coordinates. 

By comparing equation (B) with (4) it will readily appear that the centre 
of gravity of a system of equal weights placed at the angular points of a 
polygon is, in general, different from the centre of gravity of its surface, 
jrom a remark in Lardner*s Algebraic Geometry^ No, 70, it would seem 
that the author was under the impression that these points are always 
identical. It is evident, however, that this'is not generally the case, except 
when the figure is a triangle. 

The above propositions occurred to me some years ago, when I was en- 
gaged in drawing up a course of lectures on statics. I have found that 
equations (2) are not new, but the rest, as far as I know, have never been 
published. The mode of investigation seems to be as simple as could be 
desired; and, as the formulas are of some practical importance, I should 
think that they might be interesting to the junior readers of the Mathe- 
matician. 

Manchester, Dec. ith, 1846.

2^8 ON THB EQUATIONS TO SURFACES OF THE SECOND ORDER 

ivith two and only two planes parallel to a coordinate plane. iThe surface 
represented by the given equation is, consequently^ the hyperhoUnd of one 
sheet. 

The hyperboloid of one sheet has rectilinear intersection either with two 
planes parallel to a coordinate plane, or with none— the hyperbolic para- 
boloid with either one such plane or with none. This case must be dis- 
tinguished from that in which (he two planes mentioned in the first case 
coincide — when this last obtains the given surface is a cone and the above 
equation in z would have equal roots ; while io the case of the hyperbolic 
paraboloid it has essentially but one root. In the term ** plane parallel to 
a coordinate plane*' I include the coordinate pbrne itself; t.^., the case in 
which z is zero, 

The discussion m the last paragraph is not to be considered as complete. 
For instance we have not pointed out (in connection with the method of this 
paper) to h&amp;m many of the coordinate planes the rectilinear sections may 
be parallel in the case of each particular surface. On commencing the 
operations in the above example with z instead of a^ {x being isolated, and 
determined separately), it will be seen that there are in that case ino such 
planes ; and there are only two, for there are no real rectilinear intersec- 
tions parallel to the plane of x z — as will be seen on isolating y. 

I might now proceed to discuss the cases in which dii, a\ «i, s'^ Zf are (all 
or some) unreal. For instance, when the planes represented by V = and 
W = are real, and the values of z 9xe unreal and unequal, the surface is 
the hyperboloid of two sheets. 

The subject of this paper is connected with that of a question which I 
have pr(^K)eed for solution in the Lady's and Gentleman's Diary for 
1847. As the correspondents of that valuable work will probably give 
results which (in substance at least) will be equivalent to the development 
of (4), I shall say nothing upon that point, but merely suggest the desirable- 
ness of obtaining, if possible, some convenient working formuls for the dis- 
crimination of the nature of surfaces of the second degree. Nor shall I do 
more than allude to the fact that in certain cases our process becomes 
inapplicable, for instance when the squares disappear.

Now since 99 == is the equation to a tangent plane, this plane will, in 
general, meet the surface in one point only, and hence (5) will be resolvable 
into the sum of two squares. In the case, however, of the hyperboloid of 
one sheet and the hyperbolic paraboloid, the tangent plane intersects the

difference of two squares. With respect to cones and cylinders, the tangent 
plane touches along a straight line, and hence (5) must, in this case, be a 
complete square, or, which is the same thing, one of the squares into which

LXXXVIIL Mr. Fentvick. 
The equation in general of a sOrface of the second order when referred to 
any three coordinate planes, involves nine independent coefficients ; if, 
however, the planes of reference be tangential to the surface, three of the 
nine coefficients will be functions of the remaining six. It is required to 
. express the equation of thQ surface in terms of the six coefficients. 
[Solution. Mr^ O. W, Heam,'\ 
The equation to a surface of the second order touched by three given 
planes w = 0, v = 0, w = 0, is 

AV + BV + Cn?' — 2ABmi&gt; — 2 AC«w — BCi;w 
^[ax+hy + cz + \]\ 
For suppose in this equation one of the three «, », w, as for example, 
u is zero, the equation reduces to 

(Bv — Cw)'= [ax+hy-\-cz + \]\ 
or Bo — Cf»= ±[ax ^hy + cz + \], 

which together with the equation m = 0, will either represent two straight 
lines, or a point, the former when A, B, C are real, the latter when they 
are of the form a y/ — 1. 

In the former case, the surface is the hyperboloid of one sheet (including 
the cone), in the latter, any of the other surfaces of the second order.. 

That the plane w = touches the surface is manifest from the con- 
sideration that when the equation m = is combined with the equation of 
the surface, it gives either a point or two straight lines. 

It seems necessary to jsay a few words in addition as to the case of 
the cone. 

We must here consider A, B, C infinitely great compared with a, 6, c 
or 1 , so that the equation becomes 

AV + BV 4- CW — 2 ABwt? — 2 ACmw — 2BCw» = 0, 
which being homogeneous in «, v and «?, is evidently the equation to a cone 
whose vertex is determined by « = 0, t? = 0, w=- 0. Also w, t?, and m 
are tangential planes, for putting 2&lt; = 0, the equation is 

Bo — Cn? = ; 
so that « = 0, and Bt? — Cw = 0, must represent a straight line, that is, 
w = intersects the cone in one straight line, and therefore touches it. 

It must be observed, that in the foregoing I have not restricted myself to 
the condition that the planes of reference shall be the tangent planes. 
Introducing this condition we have for the equation 

Ka? + By" + Cz" — 2hBxy — 2kCxz — "IQCyz 
= [ax-^-hy + cz + XYy 
where A, B, C, a, &amp;, e are the six arbitrary constants.

XC. Mr. FenwicJc. 

Investigate the locus of the centres of surfaces of the second order 
inscribed in a given parallelopiped. 

[Solution. Mr, Thomas Weddle ; and similarly by Mr, G. W, Heam.'} 

The locus of the middle points of straight lines joining points in two 
parallel planes is a plane pandlel to and equidistant from them. Now if a 
surface of the second order touch two parallel planes, the straight line join- 
ing the points of contact will pass through the centre and be bisected in it. 
Hence, if surfaces of the second order touch two given parallel planes, the 
locus of their centres is a plane parallel to and equidistant from them. 
Applying this property to surfaces of the second order inscribed in a given 
parallelopiped, we easily see that their centres will be at the same time in 
three planes passing Uirough the intersections of the diagonals of the 
parallelopiped and parallel to its faces; that is, the point in which the 
diagonals of the parallelopiped intersect is the centre of all the surfaces of 
the second order inscribed in it. Hence the locus spoken of in this exercise 
degenerates into a point. 

Digitized by LjOOQ IC 



'266 BOLXJTIONS 07 MATHEMATICAL SXBBCISSfl. 

It easily follows, that if four planes A, B, C, D be respectively parallel 
to other four K, L, M, N, it will, in general^ be impossible to describe a 
surface of the second order to touch all these eight planes ; for, if this' be 
possible, the intersections of the diagonals of the two parallelopipeds whose 
faces are A, B, C, K, L, M, and B, C, D, L» M, N respectively, must 
coincide. ■ -

from the angles of any plane quadrilateral, is equal to the sum of the squares

XCVin. Pen^and^Inky Charlton. 

Two points and a straight line, not all in the same plane, being given, it 
is required to draw two lines from the given points to meet in the given line : 

(1) That shall make equal angles with the line ; 

(2) That shall make the greatest angle with each other. 

[Solution. Mr, Thomas Weddlcl 
Let A and B be the two given points (the student will readily construct 
the fig.), CD the given line, and first let P be a point in CD such that AP 
and BP make equal angles with the opposite portions of CD. From A 
and B draw AC and BD perpendicular to CD; let the plane BDC, be 
turned round CD until BD come into the plane ACD. and let the rotation 
be such that AC and BD may lie on opposite sides of CD. It is plain that 
BP will injthis revolution describe a right cone ; hence AP and B'P (the 
new position of BP) will make equal angles with the opposite parts of CD, 



Digitized by VjOOQ IC 



278 SOLTTTIONS OF MATHEMATICAL SXEBCISI8. 

and consequently being on different sides of CD, they must be in the same 
straight line ; also B'D is evidently perpendiculai* to CD. Hence the fol- 
lowing construction. 

From the given points A and B draw AC and BD perpendicular to the 
given line CD ; in the plane ACD, and on the side of CD opposite to AC, 
draw B'D perpendicular to CD and = BD ; join AB' intersecting CD in 
P, join also BP ; AP and BP will make equal angles with the opposite 
segments of CD. 

If the lines AP and BP are to make equal angles with the same part of 
CD, it might similarly be shown that the construction will be the very same 
except that B'D must be drawn on the same side of CD as AC. 

Next let P be such a point in CD that the angle AP'B may be a maxi- 
mum. From A and B draw AC and BC perpendicular to CD, and bisect 
CD in E. Let AE = a, BE = b, CE = DE = tf, EP = ar, and 
a» + 6&gt; ^ AB' = 2&lt;i'. Hence AF» = AC» + CP'» = AC« +{e + xy

XXXIX. Mr. W. S. B. fFoolhouse. 

(Proposed in Vol. I.. No. 5, p. 8T2.) 

In laying down fifteen points upon a plane, they may be so placed that 
sets of three points ranging exactly in a line, shall exist in troentysix 
different directions. Bequired proof. 

[SoLrxioH. By the Proposer."] 

An extreme limit may immediately be assigned to the question abstractedly 
merely by the mode in which the points must necessarily be associated. 
Each line or range exhibits a triad of points, and amongst all these triads it 
is evident that any two points cannot appear together more than once, because 
the repetition of a pair of points would imply a repetition of the same line. 
The complete number of triads that can be thus collected out of 15 things 
will comprise the whole of the 105 pairs,* and as each triad involves three 
distinct pairs, the total number of such triads is 35. We therefore conclude, 
without reference to geometrical conditions or limitations, that the greatest 
possible number of ranges cannot exceed the number 35. 

It will appear that a general discussion of the problem with the points in 
space can embrace no more possible ranges than in piano. This is obvious 

• See the Ladj's and Gentleman's Diary for 1847, page 62, solution to quettion xii.(1760.} 

Digitized by VjOOQ IC 



SOLT7TI017S OF MATH£MA.TICi.L EXEBCISSS. 279 

by considering the orthographic projection of the figure upon any plane not 
coincident with planes normal to the several lines. We shall therefore treat 
the question under its more general aspect. 

Three unequal spheres SiSiSs being taken ad libitum, both as to magnitude 
and position, it is well known that cones enveloping each pair have their 
three vertices ranging in a right line. Let the point which is the vertex of 
the cone enveloping $i8% be denoted by (I, 2); the vertex of the cone en- 
veloping s^z by (2, 3), etc. Theii the three vertices (1,2), (2, 3), (1,3) 
I'ange in a right line, which may be conveniently designated (1, 2, 3). 
Instead of three let us now take six spheres SiS-t^^ Ms^e ; then every pair of 
spheres determining a point, there will be 15 points as in the question. Also 
the points resulting from every triad of spheres constituting a range, the 
number of ranges will be 20, being the number of different triads out of six 
things. We shall therefore have 20 ranges amongst the fifleen points with 

the six spheres taken ad libitum • • • • (a). 

It remains to determine how many more ranges can possibly be deter- 
mined by varying the magnitude and position of one or more of the six spheres. 
The triads of points furnished by the 20 ranges (a) are distinguished by the 
repetition of the numerals. Thus (2, 3), (3, 5), in which 3 is repeated, are 
situated in the line (2, 3, 5) and can dierefore only be associated with the 
point (2, 5) and form one of the twenty ranges already determined. Con- 
sequently in putting down new triads of points we must in each of them 
employ all the six spheres, as no one can be repeated. It is easy therefore 
to write all the additional triads as follows :— 

(16, 23, 45), (16, 24, 35), (16, 25, 34) (A) 

(26, 13, 45), (26, 14, 35), (26, 15, 34) (B) 

(36, 12, 45), (36, 14, 25), (36, 15, 24) (C) 

(46, 12, 35), (46, 13, 25), (46, 15, 23) (D) 

{56y 12, 34), {56, 13, 24), (56, 14, 23) (E^ 

These 15 triads make up the complete set of 35 triads before mentioned, 
and any one of them may separately be converted into a new range by vary- 
ing the magnitude and position of one of the spheres. We must now ascer- 
tain how many of them can be made to range simultaneously, consistently 
with the geometrical relations of the figure. 

First take the three triads in (A) and let us direct our attention for a 
a moment to the four spheres s^^tfi^. As they comprise four different 
triads the six resulting points will have four ranges and will evidently be 
the six corners to a complete quadrilateral whose prolonged sides are the 
four lines (2, 3, 4,), (2, 3, 5), (2, 4, 5,) (3, 4, 5). The three diagonals of 
this quadrilateral are the lines joining the pairs of points (23, 45), (24, 35), 
(25, 34). According to (A) the point ( 1 , 6) is required to range in each of 
these three diagonals, which is impossible, as the three diagonals of a 
quadrilateral cannot intersect in a common point. The point (1,6) may 
however be placed on the intersection of any two of the three diagonals 
without ranging in the third. We must therefore reject at least one of the 
triads from each of the sets (A), (B), (C), (D), (E). Let the first triad be 
cancelled in each of the sets (A), (B, (C), and the ranges suggested by these 
sets will be 

(16, 24, 35), (16, 25, 34)^1 

(26, 14, 35), (26, 15,34)&gt; •• ifi) 

(36, 14, 26), (36, 15,24))

It Will be necessary lo show that the points (1,6), (2, 6), (3, 6), thus 
determined by the first five spheres, are consistent with the previously 
assigned ranges (12, 16, 2()), (13, 16, 36), (23, 26, 36). Let two lines be 
drawn, the one joining the centre of 8^ with the point (3, 5), and the other 
joining the centre of s^ with the point (3, 4). These lines being both 
situated in the plane passing through the centres of ^4^5, will intersect. 
Let the point in which they intersect be taken for the centre of another 
sphere Sj, and let its magnitude be such that the point (3, 7) may be situated 
in the line (3, 4, 5). Then since the point (3, 7) ranges with (3, 4), (4, 7) 
and also with (3, 4), (3, 5) it is evident that the point (4, 7) must coincide 
with (3, 5). Similarly the point (.5, 7) coincides with (3, 4). Therefore 
the first and second lines of (fi) may be written 

(16, 24, 47), (16, 25, 57), 
(26, 14, 47), (26, 15,57). 

Hence the points (1,6), (2, 6) determined by (/i) will respectively coincide 
with (2, 7), (1,7), and must therefore range in the same line with the point 
(1,2). The other two ranges may be similarly exhibited, and thus the 
points (1,6), (2, 6), (3, 6) which result fVom the ranges in (/3), are such aa 
to satisfy the previously implied conditions. 

The six triads contained in (D), (E) will now, every one of them, be 
individually excluded. For example take the first in (D) and it will appeSir 
that (3, 5) is required to range with (12, 46). But in (/3) it already ranges 
in (16, 24), (26, 14), two of the diagonals of the quadrilateral (1, 2, 4, 6), 
and it therefore cannot range in the tliird. The other triads in (D), (E) are 
all similarly inadmissible. 

We have therefore, in all, 26 possible ranges amongst the fifteen points. 

For the construction of the diagram the intervention of the spheres will 
not be required. Any three points (I, 2), (I, 3), (2, 3) being taken in a 
line, let three lines be drawn respectively through them, those from (1, 2)^ 
(1, 3) intersecting in (1,4), those from (1, 2), (2, 3), in (2, 4), and those 
from (1,3), (2, 3) in (3, 4). In like manner, through the same three points 
as before, let any other three lines be drawn intersecting each other in the 
poinU (I, 5), (2, 5), (3,5). By (/3) the points (1, 6), (2,6), (3, 6) are now- 
determined by the respective intersections of the diagonals therein indicated. 
The three remaining points are 
(4, 5) the mutual intersection of (14, 15), (24, 25), (34, 35), 
(4,6) „ (14, 16), (24, 26), (34, 36), 

(5, 6) „ (15, 16), (25, 26), (35, 36), (45, 46), 

and the three remaining ranges are (12, 16, 26), (13, 16, 36), (23, 26, 36). 

Since every four spheres determine a complete quadrilateral the entire 
figure will contain fifteen complete quadrilaterals, being as many as there are 
points. These quadrilaterals will have amongst them twenty different sides 
exhibiting the twenty ranges according to (a), and the other six ranges in 
(fi) will be diagonal ranges involving aaditional lines.

ON THE PLANE QUADRILATERAL. 

IMr, FenwickJ] 

PART I. 

Properties of the complete inscribed and circumscribed quadrilaterals ^ 
to a system of curves of tfhc second order. 

My chief object in view in the first part of the subsequent discussion is, 
to establish a connected system of lines and points, by which every property 
of the plane quadrilateral may readily be deduced. How far I have 
succeeded in this respect, must be left to others to determined At any rate 
I expect that I have added to the many properties already known of the 
quadrilateral and the series of conic sections by which it is necessarily 
circumscribed. 

The second part relates to properties of the centres of gravity of a 
quadrilateral and the triangles of which it is composed. Though many of 
these are known, thdr investigation, I trust, will be found interesting to 
some of our readers. When I say knonm^ I mean they are known to those 
mathematicians who read mathematical works published on the continent, 
for I am not aware that they are given in any English work. 

Produce the opposite sides of the quadrilateral ABCD to meet in E and F, 
and let O be the intersection of the diagonals AC, BD. Join EO, FO and EF.

{q) fFhen tlte four points A, B, C, D, lie in the circumference of a 
circle, the difference of the squares of the radii of the first two circles 
is equal to the difference of the squares of the radii of the other two.

Hence the following properties ; — 

(i?) If we join the centres of gravity of the four triangles which 
two and two compose a quadrilateral, we shall form another quadri- 
lateral, similar to the forme?; but reversed in position, and whose area 
is equal to one ninth of the original quadrilateral. 

{w) Also, the diagonals of the new quadrilateral which intersect 
in the centre of gravity of the original one, are parallel to the diagonals 
of the same and are equal respectively to one third of them. 

Let us now consider the centres of gravity of the four triangles formed 
by the two diagonals, and which have for their common vertex, the point of 
intersection of the diagonals.

Combine these so that the resulting line may pass through the origin^ and

The lines KM, NL, therefore, intersect in the line OQ. Hence the 
following theorems: — 

(a?) If we join the centres of gravity of the four triangles formed hy 
the two diagonals of a quadrilateral^ and which have for tlieir common 
vertesfy the point of intersection of tlie diagonals^ we sJiall form a 
parallelogram^ whose sides are parallel to the diagonals of the 
quadrilateral. 

{y) The sides of this parallelogram contain tJie centres of gravity 
of the triangles which two and two compose the quadrilateral, and its 
diagonals intersect on the line which passes through the centre of 
gravity and point of intersection of the diagonals ^ oftlie quadrilateral 

{z) The surface of this parallelogram is two ninths of the surface 
of the quadrilateral. 

By means of the centres of gravity of the eight triangles which are formed 
by the diagonals of the original quadrilateral, we can thus form a paral- 
lelogram and a new inscribed quadrilateral ; and by continuing the process 
we get a series of parallelograms and inscribed quadrilaterals, the areas of 
the latter being denoted by the sei ies 

"sT ^' W ^' 729 ^' ^'^'' 
where A is the area of the original quadrilateral. The diagonals of all the 
quadrilaterals and parallelograms intersect pn the line which passes through 
the centre of gravity and intersection of the diagonals, of the original 
quadrilateral. 

Moreover, if we join the middle points of the adjacent sides of the original 
quadrilateral, a parallelogram will be formed, having its sides parallel to the 
diagonals of the quadrilateral. And if we join the points in which the sides 
of this parallelogram cut the diagonals of the original quadrilateral, another 
quadrilateral \ill be formed similar to the original one ; and so on. These 
parallelograms and quadrilaterals have also a remarkable relation to each 
other. But we need do no more than direct the student's attention to them. 
We may remark, however, that all the diagonals of these parallelograms 
and quadrilaterals intersect, as in the former series, in the line OQ. 

Royal Military Academy, Jan: ^nd, 1847. 

J^ote hy the Editors, relative to their Elementary Propositions in 
the Geometry of Coordinates, 

We may take this opportunity to state, in reply to several inquiries, 
that many of our papers in the *' Mathematician" may be considered as 
continuations of the " Geometry of Coordinates." When the first part 
appeared, the publication of the Mathematician was not contemplated. 

Our little treatise on this subject has been translated into German by 
Dr. Wifigand, Professor of Mathematics and Natural Philosophy, at Halle. 
The following is a translation of Dr. Wiegand's preface. 

Digitized by VjOOQ IC 



i96 



ALOEDRAIC DRMONSTRATION, ETC« 



" When I first saw the Elementary Propositions, &amp;c., by W. Rutherford 
and S. Fenwick, of the Hoyal Military Academy at Woolwich, I was so 
much struck with the easy and intelligible treatment of the theorems in this 
branch of geometry, that they seemed to me well deserving to be more 
generally made known. My expectations have not been disappointed, for 
the translation of the first number has gone through two editions in a very 
short time. 

** Though the mathematical literature of Germany is by no means poor in 
comprehensive and excellent works in this particular branch, and indeed we 
may confidently say, that the works of our " Plucker" are scarcely equalled 
by all the foreign works put together, yet the important results at which he 
has arrived are still very far from being known to the mathematical world, 
in proportion to their merit. 

" My object will be fully answered if, by publishing the following papers, 
I contribute in some measure to the .circulation of new views in analytical 
investigation. Having established a direct correspondence with the 
authors, the first of whom is already well known tp mathematicians in 
Oerm^^ny, I shall l)e able to publish the subsequent numbers immediately 
after their appearance in England."

ON THE DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO EQUAL 

VOLUMES. 
IMr. Thanuu Weddle, NewcasOe-upon-Tyne,] 

The object of the following paper is the discussion of the problem, 

To divide a given ellipsoid into n equal volumes, 

and as I am not aware that this has been attempted before, I trust the 
subject will be found to possess some degree of interest. 

I shall first dispose of two or three particular solutions which it will be 
advantageous to consider independently of the general method. One of 
these solutions is as follows : 

I. Draw any diameter A'OA of the ellipsoid, and a diametral plane 
conjugate to it ; the section of the ellipsoid by this plane will be an ellipse ; 
by (D) of my paper entitled " Properties of the Ellipse," Mathematician, 
Vol. II. p. 181, divide this ellipse into n equal sectors P1OP2, PjOPs. . • • 
P«_iOP„, PnOP, ; through A'A and each of the Imes 0P„ OPj. • . .OPn, 
let a plane be made to pass, then will the ellipsoid be divided into n equal 
wedges by these n planes. 

This does not require a formal demonstration ; its truth is evident from 
the consideration that each plane section of the ellipsoid parallel to that of 
the diametral plane is divided into n equal sectors by its intersection with 
the n planes. 

Moreover the preceding construction is not confined to an entire ellipsoid ; 
it will apply to any finite portion (cut off by parallel planes) of an ellipsoid, 
an hyperboloid of one or two sheets, an elliptic paraboloid, a cone or an 
elliptic cylinder. We may here proceed in the following manner. 

r. Since the volume cut off is finite, the sections made by the parallel 
planes are necessarily ellipses ; divide either of these into n equal sectors 
PiOPj....P„OPi, (as above); find the diameter conjugate to the parallel

planes (which may be done by joining the centres 0', 0, of the sections), 
and through O'O and the lines OPi, 0P3....0Pn let planes pass, these 
will divide the solid into n equal wedges. 

It will not fail to be observed that the constructions I and V will still 
apply when the problem is to divide the solid into portidhs having given 
ratios to each other, provided the ellipse be divided into sectors having these 
ratios instead of into equal parts. These processes also hold with respect to 
portions of the solids cut off by planes passing through A' A (or O'O), the 
figure to be divided into equal parts or into parts having given ratios, being 
now a sector of an ellipse. (See (T) Mathematician, Yol II. p. 186.) 

Of course none of the preceding operations will be geometrical unless 
the division of the ellipse (or elliptic sector, as the case may be) can be 
performed geometrically. A similar remark is also applicable to some of 
the succeeding solutions. 

Before proceeding to the next method, it will be necessary to premise the 
following lemmas (a) and [p] . 

(a). In A' A any diameter of an ellipsoid, take any point P, through 
which let a plane be drawn parallel to the diametral plane conjugate to A'A : 
with the centre O as vertex and curve of section as director, let a cone be 
described ; this conical surface will cut off a solid sector which is to the 
whole ellipsoid as AP is to A'A.

We are now prepared for our second solution. 

II. Take any diameter A'A of the ellipsoid, divide it into n equal parts, 
and through the n — I points of division let planes be drawn parallel to the 
diametral plane conjugate to A'A ; with the centre as common vertex and 
the curves in which these planes cut the ellipsoid as directors, letn— I 
conical surfaces be described ; then will these surfaces divide the ellipsoid

904 OK THX BITISION OT AH XI.L|r80IB INTO EQUAL TOLtTXBS. 

into n equal parts. If A'A, instead of being divided into equal parts, be 
divided into parts having any ratios to each other, the ellipsoid will be divided 
into volumes having these ratios. 

'iliis construction, the truth of which is evident from (a, ^), is very simple 
and general ; it requires, however, the description of cones of the second 
order, so that it is not strictly geometrical. 

The preceding solution will also apply to a portion of an ellipsoid cut off 
by parallel planes, as follows : 

ir. Divide the line joining the centres of the sections into n equal parts 
or into n parts having the given ratios, and draw planes parallel to those of 
the sections and describe cones as in 11. 

A little consideration will make it evident that if a wedge be cut off from 
an ellipsoid by two planes passing through A' A, we may vary (a) by 
saying, — the corrresponding portion of the solid sector is to the wedge io 
the same ratio as before, namely, AP to A'A ; hence also, instead of {fi) we 
shall have, the portion of the wedge included between the two cones is to 
the whole wedge as PQ is to A' A. From these considerations it appears 
that the process II will effect the division of a wedge cut off by planes 
passing through the diameter employed, into equal parts, (or parts having 
given ratios),' and hence, when n is a composite number =:/^, another 
method of dividing an ellipsoid into n equal volumes may be got by com- 
bining I and II in the following manner. 

III. Divide the ellipsoid into p equal parts by I, and into q equal parts 
by II, using the same diameter A'A in both operations; then, by the 
mutual intersections of the p planes, and q — 1 cones, the ellipsoid will be 
divided into pq equal volumes. 

In the paper alluded to in I, I have virtually shown that the division of 
an ellipse into equal sectors may be made to depend on that of the circum- 
ference of a circle into equal arcs ; I shall now endeavour to establish the 
analogous property in space, namely, that the division of an ellipsoid into 
equal volumes may be effected when the surface of a sphere can be divided 
into as many equal surfaces.

case we already know one point in the plane of the second curve, only two 
corresponding points will be required to construct it ; these two points must 
•not however be diametrically opposite.

306 ON THIS DIVISION OF AN ELLIPSOID INTO SQUAL TOLVMBS. 

follows, tirith the aid of (7) , that if the curves on the ellipsoid corresponding 
to those on the sphere be found, the ellipsoid will be divided into n equal 
volumes by the description of cones having these curves for directors and the 
centre for common vertex. , Now, if the curves dividing the spherical sur- 
face be plane, that is, the arcs of either great or small circles, we know from 
what has been previously shown that the corresponding curves on the ellip- 
soid can be described, and the planes containing the latter curves will 
evidently either pass through the centre or not, according as great or small 
circles are employed. Moreover it is clear that in the former case, the 
dividing cones will consist of portions of planes only, and hence the con- 
struction will be effected geometrically, which however, cannot be done when 
small circles are used. 

Returning now to the problem which it is the object of this paper to 
discuss, we shall evidently have the following 

•OENEBAL SOLUTION. 

(A). Divide the surface of a sphere, in any manner, into n equal surfaces 
by arcs of great or small circles, find the corresponding curves on the sur- 
face of the ellipsoid, and with these curyes as directors and the centre of the 
ellipsoid as common vertex, describe cones ; these will, by their mutual inter- 
sections, divide the ellipsoid into n equal volumes. 

If small circles be excluded, the preceding process may be modified as 
follows, and it may now be termed the 

GENEBAL GEOMETRICAL SOLUTION. 

(B). Divide the surface of a sphere into n equal surfaces by the arcs of 
great circles, find the points on the ellipsoid corresponding to the angles of 
the spherical figures into which the surface of the sphere is divided, and join 
these points by straight lines in the same order as the corresponding points 
on the sphere are by the arcs of great circles ; through each of these straight 
lines and the centre of the ellipsoid, let planes be drawn ; then will the ellip- 
soid be divided into n equal volumes. 

N.B. Should any of the great circle arcs be a semicircle, the last 
construction, determining merely two diametrically opposite points in the 
corresponding curve on the ellipsoid, would be insufficient. In this case we 
must take a point in the semicircle and treat it as one of the angles of the 
spherical figures. 

It is evident that an ellipsoid may be divided into parts having given ratios 
to each other, by means of (A) and (B), providing the surface of the sphere 
be divided into parts having these ratios to each other instead of into equal 
parts. Also, if a closed curve, either contained entirely in one plane or 
consisting of portions of plane curves, be traced on the surface of an ellip- 
soid, the solid cut off by the copical surface having its vertex at the centre 
and the curve for director, may be divided into equal volumes, or into 
volumes having given ratios, providing the spherical figure bounded by the 
corresponding curve on a sphere, can be similarly divided. This includes as 
a particular case, an ellipsoidal wedge, that is, a portion of an ellipsoid cut 
off by two diametral planes, the spherical figure to be divided being a lune. 

All that now remains to he done in order to complete the solution of our 
problem, is merely to notice the most interesting methods of diidding a 
spherical surface into equal parts, and this I shall dispose of as briefly as 
possible.

ON THE DIVISION OP AN ELLIPSOID IKTO EQUAI/ VOLUMES. 307 

1 . The surface of a sphere may be divided into n equal parts as follows : 
Take any circle, either great or small, of the sphere, and inscribe in it a 
regular polygon of n sides, through the poles of this circle and each of the 
angles of the polygon, draw planes intersecting the sphere in n semicircles 
or meridians, then will the surface of the sphere be divided into n equal 
lunes. 

2. Take any diameter of the sphere, divide it into n equal parts, and 
through the n — I points of division draw planes perpendicular to the 
diameter ; these planes will divide the surface into n equal parts. (See 
Hymers's Int. Cal., third edition, p 182.) 

3. If n be a composite number =: pq^ we may combine the preceding 
constructions. Divide the surface of the sphere into/? equal parts by (1), 
and into q equal parts by (2), using in the latter operation the diameter 
passing through the poles of the circle employed in the first. The mutual 
intersections of the great and small circles will divide the surface of the sphere 
into pq equal parts. 

4. Another obvious and interesting, though very limited, method is by in- 
scribing a regular body in the sphere, and joiping the angular points by arcs 
of great circles in the same order as the edges of the body. In this way we 
may divide the surface into 4, 6, 8, 12, or 20 equal and regular spherical 
figures ; and if great circle arcs be drawn from the angles of each figure to 
the near pole of its circumscribing circle, the surface of the sphere will be 
divided into 12, 24, 24, 60, or 60 equal isosceles triangles respectively, and 
the number of divisions may be doubled by bisecting the vertical angle of 
each triangle. 

5. Divide the sphere into two hemispheres, and in each describe 
in — 1, (n even), or ^{n — 1), (n odd), spherical polygons, whose areas shall 
be equal to 0, 20, 30, etc. respectively, {0 being the n^ part of the surface 
of the sphere), and let the polygons of each hemisphere be described in such 
a manner that the m^ polygon shall contain all the preceding m— 1 poly- 
gons ; then, accounting the great circle dividing the hemispheres a polygon 
when n is even, but not when n is odd, the areas of the surfaces included 
between the polygons will be equal, and hence the surface of the sphere will 
be divided into n equal surfaces. 

The neatest mode of proceeding will be to describe regular spherical 
polygons of the same number of sides in such a way that the poles of the 
small circles circumscribing them may coincide with those of the great circle 
dividing the hemispheres. I shall now show how these polygons may be 
constructed.' 

In the first place we may observe, that if the angles of a spherical triangle 
be given, it may be described, for the sides and angles of the polar triangle 
are respectively equal to the supplements of the angles and sides of the 
primitive triangle ; hence the polar triangle may be constructed and the 
supplements of the measures of its angles will be the sides of the primitive 
triangle. 

Let it now be required to construct a regular spherical polygon of m sides 

whose area shall be 0. Suppose A6 to be a side of the polygon, and O the 

near pole of its circumscribing circle. The area of the isosceles triangle
